Spinor moving frame, type II superparticle quantization, hidden S ​ U ​ ( 8 ) SU(8) symmetry of linearized 10D supergravity, and superamplitudes
Igor Bandos, Mirian Tsulaia

Abstract
A covariant quantization of type IIB and type IIA superparticles in their spinor moving frame formulation reveals the hidden S ​ U ​ ( 8 ) SU(8) symmetry of the linearized type II supergravity. It acts on auxiliary variables parameterizing the possible choices of complex structures which is necessary to arrive at the realization of the quantum state vector as an analytic on-shell superfield, the one-particle counterpart of analytical superamplitudes for type IIB and type IIA supergravity multiplets. The description of the type IIA supergraity multiplet in terms of an analytic on-shell superfield is then identical to that for type IIB supermultiplet; the difference is in spacetime interpretation. However, the definition of suitable auxiliary variables in type IIA case requires introduction of covariantly constant S ​ O ​ ( 8 ) SO(8) vector which can be related to T-duality transform between type IIA and type IIB superspaces. The simplest analytic IIB superamplitudes discussed in the literature thus also describe type IIA processes. We elaborate on these using the spinor moving frame (Lorentz harmonic) formalism and point out the restrictions on computation of many particle type IIA amplitudes in this approach. We also briefly discuss the initial steps towards type IIA superamplitudes involving, besides supergravity multiplets, also D 0 0 -branes (Dirichlet superparticles), which was recently quantized in the similar formalism, and indicate some problems which appear are to be solved to proceed on this way.



06/03/2026, Printed August 10, 2026

Notice that the
SU(8)
R–symmetry of maximal
{\cal N}=8
supergravity is not seen in the above mentioned descriptions of D=10 and D=11 superamplitudes. One of the aims of the present paper is to
reveal hidden
SU(8)
symmetries in the on-shell superfields and superamplitudes of
D=10
type IIB and type IIA theories. The most natural way to this passes through a suitable covariant quantization of type IIB and type IIA superparticle models.

Surprisingly, to our best knowledge, the quantization of type IIB superparticle has not been elaborated yet. In this paper we address this problem in the frame of its spinor moving frame formulation. We resolve some issues of covariant quantization of type IIB superparticle, quantize in a similar manner massless type IIA superparticle and discuss how the T-duality relation between these manifests itself in our approach.

Our quantization procedure results in a state vectors described by chiral (analytical) on–shell superfield defined in superspace with complex octuplet of fermionic coordinates
\Theta^{-A}
carrying the index of fundamental representation of
SU(8)
. The description in terms of such a superfield is manifestly invariant under
SU(8)
symmetry.
This is broken–or, better to say, realized dynamically– when the explicit spacetime interpretation of the components of this on–shell superfield is carried out.

The invariance of the quantum theory of type IIB superparticle under the
SU(8)
symmetry occurs due to the presence of additional complex constrained variables
w_{q}^{A}
, carrying the index
A=1,...,8
of
SU(8)
fundamental representation and also
SO(8)
s-spinor index
q=1,...,8
. They parametrize the possible choices of the complex structure in the quantization of type IIB model. The fact that they are actually Stückelberg fields for
SU(8)
gauge symmetry reflects the fact that the choice of complex structure in quantization of type IIB superparticle does not have
physical consequences. In the case of quantization of type IIA model introduction of complex structure requires also the presence of a covariantly constant
8
-vector
k^{i}
which is necessary to connect two sets of complex constrained matrix Stückelberg fields,
w_{q}^{A}
and
w_{\dot{q}}^{A}
, the latter carrying c-spinor index of
SO(8)
,
\dot{q}=1,\ldots,8
.

Notice that the Stückelberg fields were known to be inevitable for construction of the formulations of 11D supergravity with manifest

E7
and
SU(8)
invariance
deWit:1985iy
;
deWit:1986mz
, as well as for its
E8
and
SO(16)
symmetric formulation
Nicolai:1986jk
;
Drabant:1988bk
. This was the reason of some critics and doubts in importance of such formulations. However, the 11D supergrvaity formulations of
deWit:1985iy
;
deWit:1986mz
;
Nicolai:1986jk
;
Drabant:1988bk
were proved to be useful first in studying of gaugings of lower dimensional supergravities (see e.g.
Hull:1988jw
) and then by discovery of
E7
and
E8
Exceptional field theories
Hohm:2013pua
;
Hohm:2013uia
;
Hohm:2014fxa
, which can be considered as development of this line. The same applies to very recent findings of unusual
\beta
–symmetry of type IIA and
\gamma
–symmetry of 11D supergravity
Baron:2022but
;
Baron:2025bli
.

Our study clarifies the appearance of the hidden
SU(8)
symmetry in perturbative description of
10D
type II supergravity theories and reveals its relation with introduction of complex structure in these theories. Clearly, as
E7
symmetry is realized nonlinearly on the scalar fields of supergravity multiplet, when the theory is linearized over flat (super)spacetime, only its linearly realized
SU(8)
subgroup can be seen.

We hope that our observations on hidden
SU(8)
symmetry of type IIB and type IIA on-shell superfields also will be useful for further development of superamplitude calculus in type II
10D
theories. The first steps towards such application are performed in the final Section
V
of this paper.

There we firstly elaborate
spinor moving frame based form of the spinor helicity formalism and used it for the
SU(8)
covariant description of simplest type IIB superamplitudes studied previously in
Boels:2012ie
. Then we argue that these
can be used to describe scattering process also in type IIA supergravity and indicate some restriction for the description of multiparticle processes in this formalism.

Finally we discuss a way towards more complex superamplitudes of type IIA theory involving, besides supergravity multiplets, also D
0
-brane (Dirichlet superparticle) contributions, and point out some problems which are to be solved to proceed on this way. To this end we use the spinor helicity formalism and on-shell superfield description of quantum D
0
–brane which was obtained from quantization of its spinor moving frame formulation in our recent

Bandos:2025pxv
.

The paper is organized according to the table of contents. Some technical material is moved to Appendices.

II
Spinor moving frame formulation of type IIB superparticle with SU(8) symmetry

II.1
Lagrangian mechanics of type IIB superparticle in its spinor moving frame formulation

II.1.1
Standard formulation of type IIB superparticle

Let us denote the coordinates of type IIB superspace
\Sigma^{(10|16+16)}
by
Z^{M}_{IIB}=(x^{a},\theta^{\alpha 1},\theta^{\alpha 2})
, and, following the custom of
e.g.
Zima:1984jaf
;
Galperin:2001seg
, express this by

\Sigma^{(10|16+16)}\;=\;\{Z^{M}_{IIB}\}=\{(x^{a},\theta^{\alpha 1},\theta^{\alpha 2})\}\;.

(2.1)

Here and below we use Latin letters
to denote
10
–vector indices,
a,b,c=0,1,...,9
, and Greek letters to denote
10D
Majorana–Weyl spinor indices,
\alpha,\beta,\gamma=1,...,16
. The supervielvein of flat type IIB superspace
\Sigma^{(10|16+16)}_{IIB}
,

E^{{\cal A}}=(E^{a},E^{\alpha 1},E^{\alpha 2})=dZ^{M}E^{{\cal A}}_{M}(Z)

(2.2)

can be chosen in the form of Volkov-Akulov 1-form and differentials of the fermionic coordinates,

E^{a}=\Pi^{a}=dx^{a}-id\theta^{1}\sigma^{a}\theta^{1}-id\theta^{2}\sigma^{a}\theta^{2}\;,\qquad E^{\alpha 1}=d\theta^{\alpha 1}\;,\qquad E^{\alpha 2}=d\theta^{\alpha 2}\;,\qquad

(2.3)

where
\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=\sigma^{a}_{\beta\alpha}
are
10D
generalization of the relativistic Pauli matrices. These matrices
together with their upper index counterparts
\tilde{\sigma}^{a\;\alpha\beta}=\tilde{\sigma}^{a\;\beta\alpha}
obey
the algebra

\sigma^{a}\tilde{\sigma}^{b}+\sigma^{b}\tilde{\sigma}^{a}=2\eta^{ab}{\hbox{\mybb I}}_{16\times 16}\;,\qquad

(2.4)

where
\eta^{ab}={\rm diag}(1,-1,...,-1)
is the Minkowski metric in mostly minus signature.

The supervielbein is invariant under the following type IIB supersymmetry transformations

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}x^{a}=i\theta^{1}\sigma^{a}\varepsilon^{1}+i\theta^{2}\sigma^{a}\varepsilon^{2}\;,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta^{\alpha 1}=\varepsilon^{\alpha 1}\;,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta^{\alpha 2}=\varepsilon^{\alpha 2}\;.\qquad\;

(2.5)

The superparticle worldline in type IIB superspace can be defined parametrically

{\cal W}^{1}\;\in\;\Sigma^{(10|16+16)}\;:\qquad Z^{M}_{IIB}=Z^{M}_{IIB}(\tau)=(x^{a}(\tau),\theta^{\alpha 1}(\tau),\theta^{\alpha 2}(\tau))

(2.6)

with the use of bosonic and fermionic coordinate functions
Z^{M}_{IIB}(\tau)=(x^{a}(\tau),\theta^{\alpha 1}(\tau),\theta^{\alpha 2}(\tau))
depending
on the proper time
\tau
. To simplify notation, we will denote the coordinate functions by the same symbols as coordinate; we believe that this cannot produce any confusion.

The pull–backs of the supervielbein 1–forms to the worldline are obtained by substituting the coordinate functions for the coordinates

\displaystyle E^{A}(Z(\tau))=d\tau E^{A}_{\tau}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle E^{a}_{\tau}=\dot{x}^{a}-i\dot{\theta}{}^{1}\sigma^{a}\theta^{1}-i\dot{\theta}{}^{2}\sigma^{a}\theta^{2}\;,\qquad E^{\alpha 1}_{\tau}=\dot{\theta}{}^{\alpha 1}\;,\qquad E^{\alpha 2}_{\tau}=\dot{\theta}{}^{\alpha 2}\;.\qquad

(2.7)

Here and below dot denotes the
derivative with respect to the
proper time,
\dot{Z}^{M}=\frac{dZ^{M}}{d\tau}
etc.

The type IIB (counterpart of the first order form of the) Brink–Schwarz massless superparticle action
Brink:1981nb
is constructed from pull-back of bosonic supervielbein, auxiliary momentum variable
p_{a}(\tau)
and Lagrange multiplier
e(\tau)
:

S_{BS}=\int d\tau\left(p_{a}E_{\tau}^{a}-\frac{1}{2}\,e\,p_{a}p^{a}\right)\;.

(2.8)

Besides being manifestly invariant under target (super)space supersymmetry (
2.5
), this action is also invariant under local fermionic
\kappa
–symmetry
Siegel:1983hh
3
3

3

Curiously, the counterpart of this
\kappa
–symmetry was first found for the case of massive
{\cal N}=2
superparticle in
deAzcarraga:1982dhu
published a bit earlier than
Siegel:1983hh
.

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}x^{a}=-{i}\theta^{1}\sigma^{a}\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{1}-{i}\theta^{2}\sigma^{a}\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{2}\;\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha 1}=p_{a}\tilde{\sigma}^{a\alpha\beta}\kappa^{1}_{\beta}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha 2}=p_{a}\tilde{\sigma}^{a\alpha\beta}\kappa^{2}_{\beta}\;,\qquad

(2.9)

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}p_{a}=0\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}e=-4i\dot{\theta}^{1}\kappa^{1}-4i\dot{\theta}^{2}\kappa^{2}\;.\qquad

This symmetry is important because it guarantees that the ground state of the dynamical system preserves
1/2
of the supersymmetry and hance is stable
4
4

4

It can be called
1/2
BPS where
1/2
refers to amount of preserved supersymmetry and BPS indicates that this state saturates the so-called Bogomol’nyi–Prasad–Sommerfield (or BPS) bound.
.

This fermionic gauge symmetry is infinitely reducible and does not allow for a Lorentz covariant gauge fixing
in the frame of its standard formulation
. This created problem for covariant quantization of superparticles and superstrings (where the same problem appeared, again, just in the standard formulation). The search for a solution of this problem was quite stimulating for development of the new approaches to superparticle and superstring models, including, among others, the most successful in this respect pure spinor description of quantum superparticles and superstrings by Berkovits
Berkovits:2000fe
;
Berkovits:2001rb
;
Berkovits:2006vi
, as well as a number of twistor-like formulations.
The set of these latter includes the spinor moving frame formulations of superparticles and superstrings
Bandos:1990ji
;
Bandos:1991bh
;
Bandos:1992np
;
Bandos:1992ze
;
Bandos:1996ju
;
Bandos:2007mi
;
Bandos:2007wm

which we will use in this paper
5
5

5

Actually in the case of superparticle the spinor moving frame approach was elaborated only for a simple,
{\cal N}=1
supersymmetry in different dimensions
D

Bandos:1990ji
;
Bandos:1996ju
;
Bandos:2007mi
;
Bandos:2007wm
. However, the generalization of the action and classical mechanics of the spinor moving frame formulation for
10D
type II superparticles which we will describe in a moment, is quite straightforward.
.

In the spinor moving frame formulation, the
\kappa
–symmetry of the superparticle action acquires its irreducible form. But the main reason to use this formulation in our work is that the quantization in its frame results in the higher dimensional generalization of the so-called on-shell superfields of
{\cal N}=4
SYM and
{\cal N}=4
supergrvaity (SUGRA) which are one-particle counterpart of the superamplitudes of these theories
Bianchi:2008pu
;
Brandhuber:2008pf
;
Arkani-Hamed:2008gz
;
Elvang:2009wd
;
Elvang:2015rqa
.
For the case of
10D
SYM and
11D
SUGRA the generalization of these on-shell superfields and superparticle quantization were discussed in
Bandos:2017eof
;
Bandos:2017zap
. In this paper we extend this approach for type IIB and type IIA superparticle cases and use it to to reveal the hidden
SU(8)
symmetry of linearized both
D=10
type II supergravities.

II.1.2
Spinor moving frame. Group-theoretical structure and Lorentz harmonic superspace

In the spinor moving frame formulation target superspace of the model becomes enlarged by
(a coset of)
Spin(1,9)
group parametrized by a pair of the constrained
16\times 8
matrix variables

v_{\alpha q}^{\;-},v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}
which form Spin(1,9) valued matrix

\displaystyle V_{\alpha}^{(\beta)}=\left(\begin{matrix}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+},&v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\end{matrix}\right)

\displaystyle\in

\displaystyle Spin(1,9)\;,\qquad\alpha=1,...,16\;,\qquad\dot{q}=1,...,8\;,\qquad q=1,...,8\;.\;\qquad

(2.10)

A good name for these objects
when they are
considered as coordinates of
a target superspace is
Lorenz harmonic variables
or
Lorenz harmonics
, and the superspace including these as coordinate is called
Lorentz harmonic superspace
,

\Sigma^{(10+16|16+16)}\;=\;\{{\cal Z}^{{\mathfrak{M}}}_{IIB}\}=\{(x^{a},\theta^{\alpha 1},\theta^{\alpha 2};(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+})\}\;.

(2.11)

(The first number
16
for additional bosonic variables in the notation for this superspace will be clarified below
6
6

6

Besides the constraint (
2.10
), the explanation of this number refers to certain gauge symmetries used as identification relations on the set of these constrained variables
).

When a particle (or a string) is moving through
this Lorentz harmonic superspace, these Lorentz harmonic variables give rise to worldline (or worldsheet) fields
v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}(\tau)
and
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}(\tau)
which can be called
spinor moving frame variables
due to the reasons we are going to describe below.

To streamline the presentation, in this paper we will mainly use the names ”Lorentz harmonics” and ”spinor moving frame variables”
as synonyms, applying it to both coordinates of Lorentz harmonic superspace and to their pull-back to the worldline. We make the terminological difference between Lorentz harmonics

v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
and spinor moving frame variables

v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}(\tau),v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}(\tau)
only in the places where this is essential (for instance in
footnote
7
below).

Notice that the condition (
2.10
) imposes strong constraints on the rectangular blocks of
v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}
and
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
of the matrix

V_{\alpha}{}^{\!\!\!(\beta)}
(which is called
spinor moving frame matrix
, which is rigorous
when we speak about

v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}(\tau)
and
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}(\tau)
) reducing the number of their components from 128+128=256 to 45=dim
(SO(1,9))
=dim
(Spin(1,9))
. We shall discuss a more explicit form of these constraints below, but before make some comments about group-theoretical structure of spinor moving frame variables (or Lorentz harmonics).

The splitting of the spinor moving frame matrix in (
2.10
) is invariant under local transformations of
SO(1,1)\times SO(8)
subgroup of
SO(1,9)\approx Spin(1,9)
; the group

SO(1,1)
acts by rescaling

v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\mapsto e^{\beta}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\,,\qquad v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\mapsto e^{-\beta}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}

(2.12)

and
SO(8)
gauge symmetry transforms
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
and
v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}
as an s--spinor and a c--spinor, respectively
7
7

7

When we speak about spinor moving frame variables, i.e. about fields on the worldline (or worldsheet),
\tau
–dependent

v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}(\tau)
and
v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}(\tau)
the parameter
\beta
in (
2.12
) should depend on
\tau
,
\beta=\beta(\tau)
. However, if we are speaking about coordinates of Lorentz harmonic superspace,

\beta
should be a function on
Spin(1,9)
group manifold. The same applies to the parameters of
SO(8)
transformations acting on the worldline fields and on the Lorentz harmonic superspace coordinates, respectively.

.

Using these gauge symmetries as an identification relation on the space of Lorentz harmonics, we can identify these with homogeneous coordinates of the coset

\displaystyle\frac{Spin(1,9)}{SO(1,1)\times SO(8)}\;.

(2.13)

The spinor moving frame fields of a (super)string
Bandos:1991bh
;
Bandos:1992np
;
Bandos:1992ze
are coordinate functions corresponding to this sector of Lorentz harmonic superspace, which then can be denoted by
\Sigma^{(10+16|16+16)}
in type IIB case. Schematically we can express this by

\Sigma^{(10+16|16+16)}\;=\;\{{\cal Z}^{{\mathfrak{M}}}_{IIB}\}=\{(x^{a},\theta^{\alpha 1},\theta^{\alpha 2};(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+})_{\text{mod}[SO(1,1)\times SO(8)]})\}\;

(2.14)

or simply by our original (
2.11
), but
with keeping in mind that we have to consider only superfields with definite homogeneous transformation properties
under
SO(1,1)\times SO(8)
acting on
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}
(this is to say, invariant functions and sections of fiber bundles)
8
8

8

Such type treatment originates in harmonic superspace approach to
{\cal N}=2
and
{\cal N}=3
SYM
Galperin:1984av
;
Galperin:1984bu
(probably in the form not so explicit in the original paper, but a bit reformulated and elaborated in
Bandos:1988bn
). In (
2.11
) this specific restrictions on function on harmonic superspace parametrized by
Lorentz group coordinates
(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+})
is indicated by
{}_{\text{mod}[SO(1,1)\times SO(8)]}
subscript.
. This restriction is reflected by the number of additional bosonic coordinates
16
in the notation of superspace in l.h.s. of (
2.11
)

9
9

9

Of course we could also introduce a harmonic superspace
\Sigma^{(10+45|16+16)}
with bosonic sector

{\hbox{\mybb R}}^{1+9}\times Spin(1,9)
and make restriction to superfields covariant under
SO(1,1)\times SO(8)
at the next stage, but we find more convenient the superspace including coset (
2.13
) and simplified notation
(
2.11
) for this.
.

Of course, Lorentz harmonic superspace makes sense as target superspace for supersymmetric point–like or extended objects,
p
–branes, and it is usually specific for each
p

Bandos:1994eu
. The exception are the cases of superstring
Bandos:1992np
;
Bandos:1992ze
and massless superparticle
Delduc:1991ir
;
Galperin:1991gk
;
Galperin:1992pz
for which, in the frame of above approach, one can use the same Lorentz harmonic superspace. However, for massive superparticle the Lorentz harmonic superspace is clearly different (see
Bandos:2000tg
as well as
Bandos:2025pxv
and refs. therein).

As we shall see below, in the case of a massless superparticle, the Lagrangian formulation of its spinor moving frame
formulation involves only one of two spinor frame variables,
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}(\tau)
. This implies that the model possesses
an additional
8
–parametric gauge symmetry
{\hbox{\mybb K}}_{8}
which acts by such shifts of
v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}
variables that do not break the condition (leave invariant constraints) (
2.10
), namely

\delta_{{}_{{\mathbb{K}}_{8}}}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}=0\;,\qquad\delta_{{}_{{\mathbb{K}}_{8}}}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}=K^{i}v_{\alpha p}^{\;-}\gamma^{i}_{p\dot{q}}\;,\qquad

(2.15)

where
\gamma^{i}_{p\dot{q}}=\tilde{\gamma}^{i}_{\dot{q}p}
are
SO(8)
Clebsch-Gordan coefficients (roughly speaking, SO(8) Pauli matrices) which obey

\gamma^{(i}\tilde{\gamma}^{j)}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma^{i}\tilde{\gamma}^{j}+\gamma^{j}\tilde{\gamma}^{i}\right)=\delta^{ij}\delta_{qp}\;,\qquad\tilde{\gamma}^{(i}{\gamma}^{j)}=\delta^{ij}\delta_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}\;.\qquad

(2.16)

On one hand, if we use also
{\mathbb{K}}_{8}
symmetry as an identification relation on the space of spinor moving frame variables, we can consider them as generalized (constrained) homogeneous coordinates of the coset

SO(1,9)/[(SO(1,1)\times SO(8))\subset\!\!\!\!\!\!\times{\hbox{\mybb K}}_{8}]={\hbox{\mybb S}}^{8}

(2.17)

isomorphic to eight-sphere
{\hbox{\mybb S}}^{8}
which further can be identified with celestial sphere of
10D
observer
Delduc:1991ir
;
Galperin:1991gk
.

On the other hand, it is completely legitimate to consider the spinor moving frame formulation of superparticle as superparticle moving in Lorentz harmonic superspace
with internal sector (
2.13
) and treat
{\mathbb{K}}_{8}
as an additional gauge symmetry of the action of a system moving in this superspace
10
10

10

In
D=4
such an approach was taken in
Bandos:1990ji
.
. Actually, this point of view is more convenient for our study since we use essentially the possibility to change the coordinate basis in Lorentz harmonic superspace and that looks much more natural if the latter contains the coset (
2.13
).

II.1.3
Constraints on spinor moving frame variables, moving frame vectors, Cartan forms and covariant harmonic derivative

The name spinor moving frame variables is actually motivated by the fact that the above discussed
v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}
and
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
can be considered as a kind of square roots of the vectors which form moving frame attached to the worldline of massless (super)particle (or to the worldsheet of a (super)string).

Such a moving frame can be constructed from
10
orthogonal and normalized vectors, which form the Lorentz group valued matrix

\displaystyle u_{a}^{(b)}=\left({1\over 2}\left(u_{a}^{=}+u_{a}^{\#}\right),\;u_{a}^{j}\,,{1\over 2}\left(u_{a}^{\#}-u_{a}^{=}\right)\right)\;\in\;SO^{\uparrow}(1,9)\,,\qquad\,j=1,\ldots,8.\qquad

(2.18)

Actually, we have combined one time–like and one of the spacelike vectors,
into a
light-like
u_{a}^{=}=u_{a}^{(0)}-u_{a}^{(9)}
and
u_{a}^{\#}=u_{a}^{(0)}+u_{a}^{(9)}
. This is convenient for ’attaching’ the moving frame to the worldline of a massless particle by identification of the momentum with one of the light-like vectors,
say with
u_{a}^{=}
,

p_{a}(\tau)=\rho^{\#}(\tau)u_{a}^{=}(\tau)\;.

(2.19)

Here the multiplier
\rho^{\#}(\tau)
is introduced to provide the invariance of the momentum under
SO(1,1)
symmetry acting on light–like vectors by rescaling (
cf.
(
2.12
)).

u_{a}^{=}\mapsto e^{-2\beta}u_{a}^{=}\,,\qquad u_{a}^{\#}\mapsto e^{2\beta}u_{a}^{\#}\,,\qquad u_{a}^{i}\mapsto u_{a}^{i}\,.\qquad

(2.20)

\rho^{\#}(\tau)
in (
2.19
) serves as a Stückelberg field for this gauge symmetry,

\rho^{\#}\mapsto e^{2\beta}\rho^{\#}\,.\qquad

(2.21)

The splitting in (
2.18
) is manifestly invariant under the
direct product of the above
SO(1,1)
and of the

SO(8)
symmetry acting on index
j=1,..,8
of
u_{a}^{j}
moving frame vector by its vector (v-) representation.

The spinor moving frame variables can be considered as a kind of square roots
of the moving frame vectors in the sense of the following constraints:

\displaystyle u_{a}^{=}\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=2v_{\alpha q}{}^{-}v_{\beta q}{}^{-}\;,\qquad\qquad v^{-}_{{q}}\tilde{\sigma}_{a}v^{-}_{{p}}=u_{a}^{=}\delta_{{q}{p}},\qquad

(2.22)

\displaystyle u_{{a}}^{\#}{\sigma}^{{a}}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}=2v_{{\alpha}\dot{q}}{}^{+}v_{{\beta}\dot{q}}{}^{+}\;,\qquad\qquad v_{\dot{q}}^{+}\tilde{\sigma}_{{a}}v_{\dot{p}}^{+}=u_{{a}}^{\#}\delta_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}\;.\qquad

(2.23)

\displaystyle u_{{a}}^{i}{\sigma}^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=2v_{({\alpha}|{q}}{}^{-}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{q}}v_{|{\beta})\dot{q}}{}^{+}\;,\qquad v_{{q}}^{-}\tilde{\sigma}_{{a}}v_{\dot{p}}^{+}=u_{{a}}^{i}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\;\qquad

(2.24)

which imply that

\displaystyle u_{a}^{=}u^{a=}=0\;,\qquad u_{a}^{\#}u^{a\#}=0\;,\qquad u_{a}^{=}u^{a\#}=2\;,\qquad

(2.25)

\displaystyle u_{a}^{=}u^{ai}=0\;,\qquad u_{a}^{\#}u^{ai}=0\;,\qquad u_{a}^{i}u^{aj}=-\delta^{ij}\;,\qquad

(2.26)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{abc_{1}\ldots c_{8}}u_{a}^{=}u_{b}^{\#}u_{c_{1}}^{i_{1}}\ldots u_{c_{8}}^{i_{8}}=\epsilon^{i_{1}\ldots i_{8}}\;.

(2.27)

The set of the relations (
2.25
)–(
2.27
) is equivalent to the condition (
2.18
).

The constraint (
2.22
) allows to treat the light-like moving frame vector
u_{a}^{=}
as a composite of
v_{{\alpha}{q}}^{\;-}
,
\;u_{a}^{=}=\frac{1}{8}v^{-}_{q}\tilde{\sigma}_{a}v^{-}_{q}
. Then (
2.19
) implies
p_{a}=\frac{1}{8}\rho^{\#}\,v^{-}_{q}\tilde{\sigma}_{a}v^{-}_{q}
and

\displaystyle p_{a}\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}=2\rho^{\#}\,v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}v_{\beta q}^{\;-}\;,\qquad\rho^{\#}\,v^{-}_{q}\tilde{\sigma}_{a}v^{-}_{p}=p_{a}\delta_{qp}\;.\qquad

(2.28)

These relations guarantee that the momentum
p_{a}
is light-like,
p_{a}p^{a}=0
, and provide us with straightforward
10
dimensional generalization of the famous Cartan-Penrose representation for the four–dimensional light–like vector in terms of bilinear of the bosonic Weyl spinors, one of the basic relations of twistor approach
Penrose:1972ia
;
Penrose:1986ca
;
Ferber:1977qx
;
Shirafuji:1983zd
and of the
D=4
spinor helicity formalism
Bianchi:2008pu
;
Brandhuber:2008pf
;
Arkani-Hamed:2008gz
;
Elvang:2009wd
(see
Elvang:2015rqa
for a review and more references).

Further details on spinor moving frame variables can be found in Appendix
A

II.1.4
Type IIB superparticle action in spinor moving frame formulation and its irreducible
\kappa
–symmetry

The action for massless type IIB superparticle in spinor moving frame formulation can be written in the following forms

\displaystyle S=\frac{1}{8}\int d\tau\rho^{\#}\,E^{a}_{\tau}\,v^{-}_{q}\tilde{\sigma}_{a}v^{-}_{q}\quad=\quad\int\limits_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\,E^{a}\,u_{a}^{=}\quad=\int\limits_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\,E^{=}\;.\qquad

(2.29)

Here
E^{a}=d\tau E_{\tau}^{a}
is the pull–back of type IIB Volkov-Akulov 1–form (
2.3
) and, in the second equality, we have used the constraint (
2.22
) to re-write the action in terms of composite moving frame vector
u_{a}^{=}
. Finally, in the third equality, we have used one of the covariant singlet components
E^{=}=E^{a}u_{a}^{=}
of the (pull–back of) supervielbein (
2.3
). This can be slit
in a Lorentz invariant manner
using the moving frame and spinor moving frame variables:

\displaystyle E^{=}=E^{a}u_{a}^{=}\;,\qquad E^{\#}=E^{a}u_{a}^{\#}\;,\qquad E^{i}=E^{a}u_{a}^{i}\;,\qquad

(2.30)

\displaystyle E^{-q1,2}=d\theta^{\alpha 1,2}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\;,\qquad E^{+\dot{q}1,2}=d\theta^{\alpha 1,2}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;.\qquad

(2.31)

The first form of the action (
2.29
) of spinor moving frame formulation of massless IIB superparticle makes manifest that it provides
10D
generalization of the so-called twistor–like of Ferber–Shirafuji formulation of 4D massless superparticle
Ferber:1977qx
;
Shirafuji:1983zd
. However, the bosonic spinor variables
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
involved in the action (
2.29
) (as well as in its
{\cal N}=1
counterpart
Bandos:1996ju
) are highly constrained. At the first glance, this makes the problem of its variation highly nontrivial. However, the clear group theoretical meaning of the spinor moving frame variables (Lorentz harmonics), which we have discussed in sec.
II.1.2
, helps to solve the problem.

As it is explained in Appendix
A
, the derivatives and variations of spinor moving frame variables can be expressed in terms of
SO(1,9)
Cartan forms the simplest expressions for which are

\displaystyle\Omega^{=i}:=u_{a}^{=}du^{ai}\;,\qquad\Omega^{\#i}:=u_{a}^{\#}du^{ai}\;,\qquad\Omega^{(0)}:={1\over 4}u_{a}^{=}du^{a\#}\;,\qquad\Omega^{ij}:=u_{a}^{i}du^{aj}\;.\qquad

(2.32)

These
provide a basis in the space
co-tangent to the
Spin(1,9)
group manifold.
Decomposing the differential on this manifold on this basis,

\displaystyle d^{Spin(1,9)}:=dv_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}}+d{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}}=\Omega^{(0)}D^{(0)}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{ij}D^{ij}+\Omega^{=i}D^{\#i}+\Omega^{\#i}D^{=i}\;,\qquad

(2.33)

we find expressions for the so-called covariant harmonic derivatives

\displaystyle D^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}}-v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}}\;,\qquad D^{ij}=\frac{1}{2}{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\tilde{\gamma}{}^{ij}_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha\dot{p}}^{\;+}}+\frac{1}{2}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha p}^{\;-}}\;,\qquad

(2.34)

\displaystyle D^{\#i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\tilde{\gamma}{}^{i}_{\dot{q}p}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha p}^{\;-}}\;,\qquad D^{=i}=\frac{1}{2}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}{\gamma}{}^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha\dot{p}}^{\;+}}.\qquad

(2.35)

They are given by such combinations of the usual partial derivatives of Lorentz harmonics which ’preserve’ the constraints
(
2.22
)–(
2.24
). This is tantamount to saying that these constraints are in the kernel of the covariant harmonic derivatives (
2.34
)–(
2.35
).

The action (
2.29
) is invariant under the
irreducible
version of the local fermionic
\kappa
–symmetry
(
2.9
)
11
11

11

The transformation rules
(
2.36
)
can be obtained from
(
2.9
) by using (
2.19
)
and (
2.22
), and denoting

\kappa^{+1\dot{q}}=2\rho^{\#}\kappa^{1}_{\beta}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}
and
\kappa^{+2\dot{q}}=2\rho^{\#}\kappa^{2}_{\beta}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}
.

:

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}x^{a}=-{i}\theta^{1}\sigma^{a}\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{1}-{i}\theta^{2}\sigma^{a}\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{2}\;\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha 1}=v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\kappa^{+1\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{\alpha 2}=v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\kappa^{+1\dot{q}}\;,\qquad

(2.36)

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}v^{\;-}_{\alpha q}=0=\delta_{\kappa}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}v^{\;+}_{\alpha\dot{q}}=0=\delta_{\kappa}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}=0\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\rho^{\#}=0\;.\qquad

(2.37)

Furthermore, spinor moving frame formulation allows for Lorentz invariant gauge fixing of both the above irreducible
\kappa
–symmetry and also for the worldsheet reparametrization:

\displaystyle\theta^{+1}_{\dot{q}}:=\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}=0\;,\qquad\theta^{+2}_{\dot{q}}:=\theta^{\alpha 2}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}=0\;,\qquad

(2.38)

\displaystyle X^{\#}:=x^{a}u_{a}^{\#}=\rho^{\#}\,\tau\;.\qquad

(2.39)

Indeed, it is not difficult to check that, under the
\kappa
–symmetry
(
2.36
)– (
2.37
),

\displaystyle\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{+1}_{\dot{q}}=\kappa^{+1\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\delta_{\kappa}\theta^{+2}_{\dot{q}}=\kappa^{+2\dot{q}}\;,\qquad

(2.40)

which
makes it
manifest that the Lorentz invariant conditions (
2.38
) fix the gauge with respect to the local fermionic
\kappa
–symmetry completely. The same applies to the Lorenz invariant condition (
2.39
) and reparametrization symmetry of the spinor moving frame formulation if the nondegeneracy condition
\dot{X^{a}}u_{a}^{\#}\not=0
are assumed
12
12

12

This can be checked to be consistent with the massless superparticle dynamics and are actually necessary, as this implies that

\dot{X^{a}}u_{a}^{=}
and
\dot{X^{a}}u_{a}^{i}
are expressed in terms of fermionic bilinear and thus are nilpotent.
.

II.2
Spinor moving frame action in analytical basis of type IIB Lorentz harmonic superspace

The advantage of treating the spinor moving frame formulation of superparticle as superparticle moving in Lorentz harmonic superspace (
2.11
) is the possibility to change the coordinate basis by mixing the standard superspace coordinates with Lorentz harmonics. Especially convenient is the following
analytical
coordinate basis

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(10+16|16+16)}\;=\;\{{\cal Z}^{{\mathfrak{M}}}_{An\,IIB}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(X^{=},X^{\#},X^{i}_{An}\;;\theta^{-1}_{q},\theta^{-2}_{q}\;,\theta^{+1}_{\dot{q}},\theta^{+2}_{\dot{q}}\;,(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}))\}\;,

(2.41)

\displaystyle X^{=}=x^{a}u_{a}^{=}\;,\qquad X^{\#}=x^{a}u_{a}^{\#}\;,

(2.42)

\displaystyle X^{i}_{An}=x^{a}u_{a}^{i}+i\theta^{-1}_{q}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\theta^{+1}_{\dot{p}}+i\theta^{-2}_{q}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\theta^{+2}_{\dot{p}}\;,\qquad

(2.43)

\displaystyle\theta^{-1}_{q}=\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\;,\qquad\theta^{-2}_{q}=\theta^{\alpha 2}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\;,

(2.44)

\displaystyle\theta^{+1}_{\dot{q}}=\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad\theta^{+2}_{\dot{q}}=\theta^{\alpha 2}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;.\qquad

(2.45)

As usually when defining an analytical basis
Ogievetsky:1975nu
;
Galperin:2001seg
;
Sokatchev:1985tc
;
Sokatchev:1987nk
, the choice of its coordinates is aimed to simplify the search for invariant sub–superspaces. In our case the choice of

X^{i}_{An}
in (
2.43
) is made in such a way that the invariant sub-superspace
\Sigma^{(9+16|8+8)}\subset\Sigma^{(10+16|16+16)}
is parametrized by

{\mathfrak{Z}}^{{\cal M}}=(X^{=},X^{i}_{An}\;;\theta^{-1}_{q},\theta^{-2}_{q}\;,(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}))\;.

(2.46)

Indeed, one can easily check that
\Sigma^{(9+16|8+8)}=\{{\mathfrak{Z}}^{{\cal M}}\}
is invariant under spacetime supersymmetry

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}X^{=}=2i\theta^{-1}_{q}\varepsilon^{-1}_{q}+2i\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\varepsilon^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}X^{i}_{An}=2i\theta^{-1}_{q}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\varepsilon^{+1}_{\dot{q}}+2i\theta^{-2}_{{q}}{\gamma}^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\varepsilon^{+2}_{\dot{p}}\;,\qquad

(2.47)

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta^{-1}_{q}=\varepsilon^{-1}_{q}\;,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta^{-1}_{q}=\varepsilon^{-1}_{q}\;,\qquad

(2.48)

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}=0\,,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}=0\,,\qquad

(2.49)

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad\varepsilon^{1-}_{q}=\varepsilon^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\qquad\varepsilon^{1+}_{\dot{q}}=\varepsilon^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad\varepsilon^{2-}_{q}=\varepsilon^{\alpha 2}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\qquad\varepsilon^{2+}_{\dot{q}}=\varepsilon^{\alpha 2}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;.\qquad

(2.50)

It is important that making the change of variables (
2.42
)–(
2.45
) in the action (
2.29
) we find that it depends on the pull–backs of the coordinates (coordinate functions) from invariant superspace (
2.46
) only:

\displaystyle S^{IIB}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}{\cal L}_{1}^{IIB}\;,\qquad{\cal L}_{1}^{IIB}=\rho^{\#}E^{=}=\rho^{\#}\left(DX^{=}-2iD\theta^{-1}_{q}\,\theta^{-1}_{q}-2iD\theta^{-2}_{q}\,\theta^{-2}_{q}-\Omega^{=i}X_{An}^{i}\right)=:d\tau L^{IIB}\;.\qquad

(2.51)

Here the covariant derivatives are defined with the use of the composite
SO(1,1)
and
SO(8)
connection given by Cartan forms
\Omega^{0}
and
\Omega^{ij}
(
2.32
) (
cf.
Eqs. (
A.19
)–(
A.22
)) in Appendix
A
),

\displaystyle DX^{=}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle dX^{=}+2X^{=}\Omega^{(0)}\;,\qquad

(2.52)

\displaystyle D\theta^{-1}_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\theta^{-1}_{q}+\Omega^{(0)}\theta^{-1}_{q}+{1\over 4}\Omega^{ij}\theta^{-1}_{p}\gamma_{{p}{q}}^{ij}\;,\qquad

(2.53)

\displaystyle D\theta^{-2}_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\theta^{-2}_{q}+\Omega^{(0)}\theta^{-2}_{q}+{1\over 4}\Omega^{ij}\theta^{-2}_{p}\gamma_{{p}{q}}^{ij}\;.\qquad

(2.54)

The 1 bosonic and
8+8=16
fermionic coordinates (coordinate functions) absent in the actions are
X^{\#}
,
\theta^{+1}_{\dot{q}}
and
\theta^{+2}_{\dot{q}}
which are transformed additively by (are Stückelberg fields for) reparametrizations and local fermionic kappa–symmetry (
2.36
), respectively.
Their disappearance after changing coordinate basis can be treated as automatic gauge fixing of these symmetries.

II.3
Hamiltonian mechanics

II.3.1
Canonical momenta and primary constraints

In the central coordinate basis (
2.11
) the canonical momenta conjugate to usual bosonic and fermionic coordinate functions (
2.6
) are defined by

P_{M}:=(p_{a},\pi_{\alpha}^{1},\pi_{\alpha}^{2})=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{Z}^{M}}\equiv\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial d{Z}^{M}}\;.

(2.55)

For the Lagrangian of the spinor moving frame action (
2.29
),

{\cal L}_{1}=d\tau L=\rho^{\#}E^{a}u_{a}^{=}=\frac{1}{8}\rho^{\#}v^{-}_{q}\tilde{\sigma}_{a}v^{-}_{q}E^{a}\;,\qquad

(2.56)

calculation of these momenta result in the following set of primary constraints

\displaystyle\mathfrak{f}_{a}=p_{a}-\rho^{\#}u_{a}^{=}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(2.57)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha}^{1}=\Pi_{\alpha}^{1}+ip_{a}\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}\theta^{\beta 1}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(2.58)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha}^{2}=\Pi_{\alpha}^{2}+ip_{a}\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}\theta^{\beta 2}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(2.59)

Notice that the constraint (
2.57
) reproduces (
2.19
) and hance, due to the constraint (
2.22
), also
the
10D
generalization (
2.28
) of the Cartan–Penrose representation for
4D
light-like momentum.

The Poisson brackets for coordinate functions are defined as graded–antisymmetric linear operation with the following basic relations

\displaystyle{}[P_{M}\,,Z^{N}\}_{{}_{P.B.}}=-(-)^{\epsilon(M)\,\epsilon(N)}[Z^{N}\,,P_{M}\}_{{}_{P.B.}}=-\delta_{M}^{N}\;,\qquad{}\qquad[Z^{M}\,,Z^{N}\}_{{}_{P.B.}}=0\;,\qquad[P_{N}\,,P_{M}\}_{{}_{P.B.}}=0\;,\qquad

(2.60)

where
\epsilon(M):=\epsilon(Z^{M})
is Grassmann parity of the coordinates (also called fermionic parity):

\epsilon(a):=\epsilon(x^{a})=0
,
\epsilon(\alpha)=:\epsilon(\theta^{\alpha 1,2})=1
.
Using (
2.60
) we easily find the following algebra of fermionic constraints

\displaystyle{}[\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha}^{1}\,,\mathfrak{d}_{\beta}^{1}\}_{{}_{P.B.}}=-2ip_{a}\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}\approx-4i\rho^{\#}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}v_{\beta q}^{\;-}\;,\qquad

(2.61)

\displaystyle{}[\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha}^{1}\,,\mathfrak{d}_{\beta}^{2}\}_{{}_{P.B.}}=0\;,\qquad

(2.62)

\displaystyle{}[\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha}^{2}\,,\mathfrak{d}_{\beta}^{2}\}_{{}_{P.B.}}=-2ip_{a}\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}\approx-4i\rho^{\#}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}v_{\beta q}^{\;-}.\qquad

(2.63)

Appearance of the
16\times 16
matrix of rank
8
in the r.h.s.-s of (
2.61
) and (
2.63
) clearly indicates that each of
\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha}^{1}
and
\mathfrak{d}_{\alpha}^{2}
is the mixture of the second class and first class constraints. (These latter are the generators of the
\kappa
-symmetry (
2.36
)
in the frame of Hamiltonian formalism by Dirac
Dirac:1963
).

Neither (
2.57
) is the first class constraint, despite
its Poisson brackets with fermionic constraints vanish. To see this, we should take into account one more primary constraint,

P^{(\rho)}_{\#}:=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{\rho}^{\#}}\equiv\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial d\rho^{\#}}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(2.64)

Its Poisson brackets with (
2.57
),
{}[\mathfrak{f}_{a},P^{(\rho)}_{\#}]_{P.B.}=-u_{a}^{=}
shows that it forms the pair of conjugate second class constraint with
u^{a\#}\mathfrak{f}_{a}
.

To proceed we need to calculate also the momenta for the spinor moving frame variables. Their canonical momenta and nonvanishing basic Poisson brackets can be defined similarly to (
2.55
),

\displaystyle P_{-q}^{\;\alpha}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{v}_{\alpha q}^{\;-}}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial dv_{\alpha q}^{\;-}}\;,

\displaystyle\;\qquad P_{+\dot{q}}^{\;\alpha}=\frac{\partial L}{\partial\dot{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial dv_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}}\;,\qquad

(2.65)

\displaystyle{}[P_{-q}^{\;\alpha}\,,\,v_{\beta q}^{\;-}]_{{}_{P.B.}}=-[v_{\beta p}^{\;-}\,,\,P_{-q}^{\;\alpha}]_{{}_{P.B.}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\delta^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}\delta_{qp}\;,\qquad

(2.66)

\displaystyle{}[P_{+\dot{q}}^{\;\alpha}\,,\,v_{\beta\dot{p}}^{\;+}]_{{}_{P.B.}}=-[v_{\beta\dot{p}}^{\;+}\;,\;P_{+\dot{q}}^{\;\alpha}]_{{}_{P.B.}}=-\delta^{\alpha}{}_{\beta}\delta_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}\;.\qquad

(2.67)

Calculation of the canonical momenta (
2.65
) with the Lagrangian form (
2.56
) clearly gives the constraints

P_{-q}^{\;\alpha}\approx 0\qquad\text{and}\qquad P_{+\dot{q}}^{\;\alpha}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(2.68)

If working
with these canonical momenta and
these constraints, one should also consider the defining relations of the spinor moving frame variables (
2.22
)–(
2.24
) (Lagrangian constraints) as constraints in the Hamiltonian formalism. The number of independent relations among these Lagrangian constraints is clearly

256-45=211
. Putting aside a nontrivial problem how
to describe these independent constraints hidden in (
2.22
)–(
2.24
)
13
13

13

See
Bandos:1993yc
for discussing this problem for the case of spinor moving frame variables for M2–brane, that time called by its original name
11D
supermembrane.
, this should be canonically conjugate to some
211
linear combinations of the constraints (
2.68
). Then, in principle, one can find these
211
constraints, pass to the suitable Dirac brackets and consider these
211
constraints coming from (
2.68
) as well as (
2.22
)–(
2.24
) as strong relation, this is to say as the relations which can be used before calculating all the Dirac brackets
Dirac:1963
.

Fortunately there exists a shortcut which allows to avoid the above described very technically involving way. It consists in using, instead of canonical momenta (
2.65
), only the so-called covariant momenta of the spinor moving frame variables.

II.3.2
Covariant momenta of spinor moving frame variables

Covariant momenta for spinor moving frame variables can be expressed through canonical momenta
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}P_{+\dot{q}}^{\alpha}-v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}P_{-q}^{\alpha}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{ij}=\frac{1}{2}{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\tilde{\gamma}{}^{ij}_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}P_{+\dot{p}}^{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}P_{-p}^{\alpha}\;,\qquad

(2.69)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\tilde{\gamma}{}^{i}_{\dot{q}p}P_{-p}^{\alpha}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{=i}=\frac{1}{2}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}{\gamma}{}^{i}_{q\dot{p}}P_{+\dot{}}^{\alpha}\;.\qquad

(2.70)

Their characteristic property is that they have vanishing Poisson brackets with all the constraints (
2.22
)–(
2.24
).
Thus the above described Dirac brackets (if constructed) would coincide with their Poisson brackets and, hence, the constraints (
2.22
)–(
2.24
) can be considered as strong equalities if only the covariant momenta (
2.69
)–(
2.70
) are used.

These covariant momenta are ”classical counterparts” of the covariant harmonic derivatives (
2.34
)–(
2.35
). Particularly, their quantum version in coordinate representation is given by operators proportional to these covariant derivatives.
They can be obtained by decomposing the canonical 1-form of the spinor moving frame sector of the phase (super)space on the Cartan forms (
cf.
(
2.33
))
14
14

14

The r.h.s. of (
2.71
) can be obtained from its l.h.s. by using the admissible derivatives of
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
and
{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}
, Eqs. (
A.19
) and (
A.20
).

\displaystyle dv_{\alpha q}^{\;-}P_{-q}^{\alpha}+d{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}P_{+\dot{p}}^{\alpha}=\Omega^{(0)}\mathfrak{d}{}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{ij}\mathfrak{d}^{ij}+\Omega^{=i}\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}+\Omega^{\#i}\mathfrak{d}^{=i}\;.\qquad

(2.71)

which, in the light of (
2.65
) is tantamount to saying that they can be obtained by taking derivative of the Lagrangian one-form with respect to Cartan 1-forms,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}{}^{(0)}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial\Omega^{(0)}}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{ij}=-\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial\Omega^{ij}}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial\Omega^{=i}}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{=i}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial\Omega^{\#i}}\;.\qquad

(2.72)

The calculation of these covariant momenta using the Lagrangian form of the spinor moving frame formulation of superparticle in central coordinate basis, Eq. (
2.56
) results in the constraints

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}{}^{(0)}\approx 0\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{ij}\approx 0\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}\approx 0\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{=i}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(2.73)

These simple set of constraints however does not imply that all the spinor moving frame variables are pure gauge (Stückelberg) fields, because not all of these constraints are of the first class.

We will not study further the Hamiltonian mechanics of the type IIB massless superparticle in the central basis of Lorentz harmonic superspace because the experience (of e.g.
Bandos:2024hnj
and refs. therein) suggests that the problem can be essentially simplified by passing to the analytical basis described in sec.
II.2
.

II.3.3
Hamiltonian mechanics in the analytical basis

In the analytical basis of Lorentz harmonic superspace the configuration space of our dynamical system is automatically reduced and include
the subset of coordinate functions corresponding to the coordinates of invariant sub–superspace

\Sigma^{(9+16|8+8)}
of Eq. (
2.46
),

{\mathfrak{Z}}^{{\cal M}}(\tau)=\{(X^{=}(\tau),X^{i}_{An}(\tau)\;;\theta^{-1}_{q}(\tau),\theta^{-2}_{q}(\tau)\;,(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}(\tau)\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}(\tau)))\}\;,\qquad

(2.74)

Lagrange multiplier
\rho^{\#}(\tau)
,
and
their canonical and covariant momenta,

\displaystyle P_{=}:=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial dX^{=}}\;,\qquad P_{i}:=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial dX^{i}_{An}}\;,\qquad\Pi^{1-}_{q}:=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial d\theta^{-1}_{q}}\;,\qquad\Pi^{2-}_{q}:=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial d\theta^{-2}_{q}}\;,\qquad P^{(\rho)}_{\#}:=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial d\rho^{\#}}\;,\qquad

(2.75)

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}{}^{(0)}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial\Omega^{(0)}}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{ij}=-\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial\Omega^{ij}}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial\Omega^{=i}}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{=i}=\frac{\partial{\cal L}_{1}}{\partial\Omega^{\#i}}\;.\qquad

(2.76)

The derivatives in (
2.76
) are taken with keeping fixed

X^{=},X^{i}_{An},\theta^{-1}_{q},\theta^{-2}_{q}
.

The above definitions also imply the following form of the Legendre transform from the Lagrangian to (almost) canonical Hamiltonian

\displaystyle d\tau H=dX^{=}P_{=}+dX^{i}_{An}P_{i}+d\theta^{-1}_{q}\Pi^{1}_{-q}+d\theta^{-2}_{q}\Pi^{2}_{-q}+d\rho^{\#}P^{(\rho)}_{\#}+\Omega^{(0)}\mathfrak{d}{}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{ij}\mathfrak{d}^{ij}+\Omega^{=i}\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}+\Omega^{\#i}\mathfrak{d}^{=i}-{\cal L}_{1}\;.\qquad

(2.77)

With the Lagrangian form (
2.51
), the calculation of all the canonical and covariant momenta result in the constraints. It is convenient to arrange them in the following sequence

\displaystyle\rho^{\#}-p_{=}\approx 0\;,\qquad P^{(\rho)}_{\#}\approx 0\;,

(2.78)

\displaystyle X^{i}_{An}+\frac{1}{\rho^{\#}}\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}\approx 0\;,\qquad p_{i}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(2.79)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{1}_{-q}=\Pi^{1}_{-q}+2ip_{=}\theta^{-1}_{q}\approx 0\;,

(2.80)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{2}_{-q}=\Pi^{2}_{-q}+2ip_{=}\theta^{-2}_{q}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(2.81)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}-2X^{=}p_{=}-\theta^{-1}_{q}\Pi^{1}_{-q}-\theta^{-2}_{q}\Pi^{2}_{-q}\approx 0\;,

(2.82)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{ij}-\frac{1}{4}\theta^{-1}\gamma^{ij}\Pi^{1}_{-}-\frac{1}{4}\theta^{-2}\gamma^{ij}\Pi^{2}_{-}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(2.83)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{=i}\approx 0\;.

(2.84)

Eqs. (
2.78
) and (
2.79
) are pairs of explicitly solved second class constraints which hance can be used to reduce the phase space of our dynamical system by removing

(\rho^{\#},P_{\#}^{(\rho)})
and
(X_{An}^{i},p_{i})
pairs of canonically conjugate variables.
The constraints (
2.82
)– (
2.84
) are of the first class
15
15

15

The first class constraints (
2.82
) and (
2.83
) are the linear combination of the results of straightforward calculations of the covariant momenta and the fermionic second class constraints (
2.80
) and (
2.81
).
. They generate
SO(1,1)
,
SO(8)
and

{\hbox{\mybb K}}_{8}
gauge symmetries, respectively, on the reduced phase space.

Finally, (
2.80
) and (
2.81
) are the second class fermionic constraints which are imaginary and have nondegenerate Poisson brackets with themselves,

\displaystyle\{\mathfrak{d}^{1}_{-q},\mathfrak{d}^{1}_{-p}\}_{PB}=-4ip_{=}\delta_{qp}\;,\qquad\{\mathfrak{d}^{1}_{-q},\mathfrak{d}^{2}_{-p}\}_{PB}=0\;,\qquad\{\mathfrak{d}^{2}_{-q},\mathfrak{d}^{2}_{-p}\}_{PB}=-4ip_{=}\delta_{qp}\;.\qquad

(2.85)

(The logic beyond using for these constraints the same symbol
\mathfrak{d}
as for covariant momenta of spinor moving frame variables is that they are actually classical counterpart of fermionic
covariant
derivatives, see Eqs. (
3.6
) –(
3.7
) below).
To proceed with the massless type IIB superparticle quantization in
its spinor moving frame formulation, we should decide how to deal with these constraints.

Let us note that the canonical Hamiltonian (
2.77
) vanishes on the surface of constraints, as it should be for any reparametrization-invariant theory.

II.3.4
Complex structure of type IIB superspace, conversion vs. Gupta –Bleuler,
and prequel of the hidden SU(8) symmetry

One of the ways to deal with the above real octuplets of fermionic constriants is to combine them in two complex octuplets related by complex conjugation,

\displaystyle\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left({\mathfrak{d}}^{1}_{-q}+i{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}_{-q}\right)\,\;,\qquad{\mathfrak{d}}_{-q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left({\mathfrak{d}}^{1}_{-q}-i{\mathfrak{d}}^{2}_{-q}\right)=-(\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-q})\,\;,\qquad q,p=1,\ldots,8\;.

(2.86)

These obey (see (
2.85
))

\displaystyle\{\mathfrak{d}_{-q},\mathfrak{d}_{-p}\}_{PB}=0\;,\qquad\{\mathfrak{d}_{-q},\bar{\mathfrak{d}}^{p}_{-}\}_{PB}=-4ip_{=}\delta_{q}{}^{p}\;,\qquad\{\bar{\mathfrak{d}}^{q}_{-},\bar{\mathfrak{d}}^{p}_{-}\}_{PB}=0\;\qquad

(2.87)

which allows to make the
conversion
of the second class constraints into the first class one and to use
Gupta-Bleuler procedure
in quantization.

The
conversion method
consists in skipping one of two conjugate second class constraints, e.g.
\mathfrak{d}_{-q}
in our case, thus converting the other,
\bar{\mathfrak{d}}^{p}_{-}
in our case, into the first class constraint
Faddeev:1986pc
;
Egorian:1993sc
;
Batalin:1989dm
. The
Gupta–Bleuler method
(see e.g.
deAzcarraga:1982dhu
and refs. therein) implies making the same at the stage of quantization, namely
imposing as a condition on the state vector the quantum version of only one of two canonically conjugate second class constraints. When this is related to the other one by hermitian conjugation, the matrix elements of both quantum constraints between physical states vanish.

Notice that the conversion method is more general as, in principle, it can be used also for canonically conjugate second class constraints which are not related by complex conjugation: rather one of two conjugate second class constraints is considered as a gauge fixed condition for the gauge symmetry generated by the new first class constraints created by the conversion procedure.

The above complex constraints can be written in terms of complex fermionic coordinates of the reduced superspace

\Theta^{-q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\theta^{1-}_{q}+i\theta^{2-}_{q}\right)\;,\qquad\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\theta^{1}_{q}-i\theta^{2}_{q}\right)\;,\qquad q,p=1,\ldots,8\;,

(2.88)

and their conjugate momenta

\Pi_{-q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\Pi^{1}_{-q}-i\Pi^{2}_{-q}\right)\;,\qquad\bar{\Pi}{}^{-q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\Pi^{1}_{-q}+i\Pi^{2}_{-q}\right)\;,\qquad q,p=1,\ldots,8\;,

(2.89)

namely

\displaystyle\bar{{\mathfrak{d}}}^{-q}=\bar{\Pi}{}^{-q}+2ip_{=}\Theta^{-q}\approx 0\;,\qquad{\mathfrak{d}}_{-q}={\Pi}_{-q}+2ip_{=}\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{q}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(2.90)

Notice that we have begun to write
upper and lower indices
q,p
on
the complex fermionic coordinates, momenta and constraints. This is the prequel of the discussion on the
SU(8)
symmetry of type IIB supergravity which is hidden in the Lagrangian (
2.51
) when written in terms of real fermionic variables. The indications of this hidden invariance will be even more manifest after the quantization, if we perform this in terms of the complex fermionic coordinates (
2.88
). However, the classical Lagrangian written in terms of these variables does not possess such symmetry. In the next (sub)section we shall discuss the gauge equivalent version of the model which does possess the manifest
SU(8)
symmetry.

II.4
Massless type IIB superparticle mechanics in the analytical basis with manifest complex structure and SU(8) symmetry

II.4.1
SU(8)/SO(8) coset and complex structure

To make
SU(8)
symmetry manifest at the level of the classical Lagrangian, we can introduce new variables which
parametrize the space of possible complex structures.
These can be introduced in the reduced type IIB Lorentz harmonic superspace (
2.74
). These are described by
SU(8)
valued 8
\times 8
complex matrix
{w}_{q}{}^{A}=(\bar{{w}}_{qA})^{*}
.

\displaystyle{w}_{q}{}^{A}\bar{{w}}_{pA}=\delta_{qp}\;,\qquad\bar{{w}}_{qA}{w}_{q}^{B}=\delta_{A}{}^{B}\;,\qquad{\rm det}({w}_{q}{}^{A})=1\;,\qquad

(2.91)

which are transformed by
SO(8)
on its
p,q
indices. The complex fermionic coordinates can be introduced as

\Theta^{-A}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\theta^{1-}_{q}+i\theta^{2-}_{q}\right){w}_{q}{}^{A}\;,\qquad\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\theta^{1}_{q}-i\theta^{2}_{q}\right)\bar{{w}}_{qA}\;,\qquad A,B=1,\ldots,8\;.

(2.92)

The original guess for complex coordinates, (
2.88
), is reproduced by setting

\bar{{w}}_{qA}=\delta_{qA}={w}_{q}{}^{A}
.

In the generic case, as
\theta^{1}_{q}
and
\theta^{2}_{q}
are transformed by local
SO(8)
symmetry, which is one of the characteristic gauge symmetries acting on the spinor moving frame variables
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
, the newly introduced
\bar{w}_{qA}
and
{w}_{q}{}^{A}
should be worldline fields rather than constant matrices. Then the
SU(8)
symmetry acting on their
A,B
indices by its fundamental representation can also be made local. Actually this local
SU(8)
will be a gauge symmetry of the Lagrangian form which we obtain below. Of course, in this case
\bar{w}_{qA}
and
{w}_{q}{}^{A}
providing the bridge between
SU(8)
and
SO(8)
representations become pure gauge or Stückelberg fields. This fact reflects the freedom in the choice of complex structure in the (reduced) type IIB Lorentz harmonic superspace.

Hence we can conclude that the
SU(8)
symmetry manifests itself when the model is described in (is reduced to) the form in which the explicit choice of complex structure is not essential. We will see that exactly this happens with our model after a suitable quantization.

The
SO(8)
invariant Cartan form which plays the role of
SU(8)
connection is defined with the use of
SO(8)
covariant derivatives of our bridge variables,

\displaystyle{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}=\bar{{w}}_{qB}D{w}_{q}{}^{A}=\bar{{w}}_{qB}d{w}_{q}{}^{A}-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}\bar{{w}}_{qB}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}{w}_{p}{}^{A}\;.\qquad

(2.93)

This implies

\displaystyle D{w}_{q}{}^{A}=d{\rm w}_{q}{}^{A}-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}{w}_{p}{}^{A}={w}_{q}{}^{A}{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\;,\qquad D\bar{{\rm w}}_{qB}=d\bar{{\rm w}}_{qB}-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}\bar{{\rm w}}_{pB}=-{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\bar{{\rm w}}_{qA}\;.\qquad

(2.94)

These equations can be written in terms of a complete
SU(8)\times SO(8)
covariant derivatives
as covariant constancy of the bridge variables
16
16

16

These conditions provide an equivalent form of the definition of the composite
SU(8)
connection (
2.93
) similarly to how the covariant constancy of metric identifies affine connection with Christoffel symbol in General Relativity.
,

\displaystyle{\cal D}{w}_{q}{}^{A}=d{w}_{q}{}^{A}-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}{w}_{p}{}^{A}-{w}_{q}{}^{A}{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}=0\;,\qquad{\cal D}\bar{{w}}_{qB}=d\bar{{w}}_{qB}-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}\bar{{w}}_{pB}+{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\bar{{w}}_{qA}=0\;.\qquad

(2.95)

Notice that the matrix 1–form (
2.93
) is traceless,
\mho_{A}{}^{A}=0
, due to the third condition in (
2.91
). More details on the composite connection

\mho_{A}{}^{B}=0
can be found in Appendix
A.4
.

Covariant derivatives in Lorentz harmonic superspace complemented by
SU(8)
group manifold parametrized by coordinates
\bar{w}_{qA},w_{q}^{A}
are defined by decomposition

\displaystyle d^{v,w,\bar{w}}\equiv d^{Spin(1,9)\otimes SU(8)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle dw_{q}^{A}\partial_{qA}+d\bar{w}_{qA}\bar{\partial}_{q}^{A}+dv_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}}+d{v}_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}}=

(2.96)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}D_{A}{}^{B}+\Omega^{(0)}D^{(0)}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{ij}\tilde{D}{}^{ij}+\Omega^{=i}D^{\#i}+\Omega^{\#i}D^{=i}

where the differential operators

\displaystyle D_{A}{}^{B}=w_{q}^{B}\partial_{qA}-\bar{w}_{qA}\bar{\partial}_{q}^{B}\;,

(2.97)

\displaystyle\text{and}\qquad\tilde{D}{}^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{D}{}^{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left((w^{A}\gamma^{ij})_{q}\partial_{qA}+(\bar{w}_{A}\gamma^{ij})_{q}\bar{\partial}_{q}^{A}\right)

(2.98)

provide representations of
SU(8)
and
SO(8)
groups and
D^{(0)}
,
D^{ij}
,
D^{\#i}
and
D^{=i}
are covariant derivatives of Lorentz harmonic variables defined in (
2.34
)–(
2.35
).
Now these are also
covariant derivatives in the Lorentz harmonic superspace enlarged (with respect to (
2.14
)) by
SU(8)
group manifold.

II.4.2
Lagrangian of type IIB superparticle with manifest
SU(8)
symmetry

Substituting the expressions for real fermionic coordinates

\displaystyle\theta^{1-}_{q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}{w}_{q}{}^{A}+\Theta^{-A}\bar{{w}}_{qA}\right)\;,\qquad\theta^{2-}_{q}=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}{w}_{q}{}^{A}-\Theta^{-A}\bar{{w}}_{qA}\right)\;\qquad

(2.99)

into
(
2.51
), we find the Lagrangian form in terms of complex coordinates:

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{1}^{IIB}=\rho^{\#}E^{=}=\rho^{\#}\left(DX^{=}-2i{\cal D}\Theta^{-A}\,\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}+2i\Theta^{-A}{\cal D}\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}\,-\Omega^{=i}X_{An}^{i}\right)\;,\qquad

(2.100)

where

\displaystyle{\cal D}\Theta^{-A}:=d\Theta^{-A}+\Omega^{(0)}\Theta^{-A}-\Theta^{-B}{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\;,\qquad{\cal D}\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{B}:=d\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{B}+\Omega^{(0)}\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}+{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}

(2.101)

are
SU(8)\times SO(1,1)
covariant derivatives (
cf.
(
2.95
) and (
2.53
)). Actually, the terms with
\Omega^{(0)}
coming from two covariant derivatives in (
2.100
) cancel, so that effectively
\Omega^{(0)}
defined in (
A.6
) does not enter thie Lagrangian form. We however included it in (
2.101
) to make derivative covariant with respect to all symmetries of the model.

Besides the manifest
SU(8)\times SO(8)\times SO(1,1)
gauge symmetry, the Lagrangian 1–form (
2.100
)
is also invariant under rigid
U(1)
symmetry which originates in
SO(2)
invariance of the massless IIB superparticle in its standard formulation (but broken by type IIB superstring).

II.4.3
SU(8)
invariant form of the Hamiltonian mechanics

The Legendre transform of the Lagrangian form (
2.100
) defining its corresponding canonical Hamiltonian

\displaystyle d\tau H=dX^{=}P_{=}+dX^{i}_{An}P_{i}+d\theta^{-1}_{q}\Pi^{1}_{-q}+d\theta^{-2}_{q}\Pi^{2}_{-q}+d\rho^{\#}P^{(\rho)}_{\#}+\Omega^{(0)}\mathfrak{d}{}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{2}\Omega^{ij}\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{ij}+\Omega^{=i}\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}+\Omega^{\#i}\mathfrak{d}^{=i}+

\displaystyle+{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\mathfrak{d}_{B}{}^{A}-{\cal L}_{1}\;,\qquad

(2.102)

differs from (
2.77
) by additional term
{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\mathfrak{d}_{B}{}^{A}
,
which contains a new covariant momentum

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}{}_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w_{q}^{B}P^{w}_{qA}-\bar{w}_{qA}\bar{P}{}^{\bar{w}}{}_{q}^{B}\;,\qquad

(2.103)

and by modification of the covariant momentum of spinor moving frame variables generating
SO(8)
symmetry

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}{}^{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left((w^{A}\gamma^{ij})_{q}\mathfrak{d}_{qA}+(\bar{w}_{A}\gamma^{ij})_{q}\bar{\mathfrak{d}}_{q}^{A}\right)\;.

(2.104)

This has non vanishing Poisson brackets also with the new variables
w_{q}^{A}
and
\bar{w}_{qA}
.

The set of constraints, which follows from the calculation of
the canonical and covariant momenta, includes, as before, explicitly resolved pairs of second class constraints (
2.78
) and (
2.79
) which we use to reduce the phase space by omitting
\rho^{\#}
,
X^{i}_{An}
directions in the configuration space and their canonical momenta.

After some algebra, the remaining set of constraints
can be separated in the first class constraints

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}-2X^{=}p_{=}-\Theta^{-A}\Pi_{-A}-\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}\bar{\Pi}{}^{A}_{-}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(2.105)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{ij}\approx 0\;,\qquad

(2.106)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{=i}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(2.107)

generating
SO(1,1)
,
SO(8)
and
{\hbox{\mybb K}}_{8}
gauge symmetry, respectively,
the first class constraint

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}{}_{B}{}^{A}+\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{B}\bar{\Pi}{}^{A}_{-}-\Theta^{-A}\Pi_{-B}-\frac{1}{8}\left(\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{C}\bar{\Pi}{}^{C}_{-}-\Theta^{-C}\Pi_{-C}\right)\approx 0\;,\qquad

(2.108)

generating
SU(8)
gauge symmetry, as well as fermionic second class constraints

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{-A}=\Pi_{-A}+2ip_{=}\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}\approx 0\;,\qquad\bar{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{A}_{-}=\bar{\Pi}{}^{A}_{-}+2ip_{=}\Theta^{-A}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(2.109)

The canonical Hamiltonoian vanishes as it should be for the theory with reparametrization invariance.

Constraints (
2.109
) obey the
d=1
,
n=8
supersymmetry algebra with
SU(8)
automorphism group.

\displaystyle\{\mathfrak{d}_{-A},\mathfrak{d}_{-A}\}_{PB}=0\;,\qquad\{\mathfrak{d}_{-A},\bar{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{B}_{-}\}_{PB}=-4ip_{=}\delta_{A}{}^{B}\;,\qquad\{\bar{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{A}_{-},\bar{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{B}_{-}\}_{PB}=0\;.\qquad

(2.110)

For these constraints we will use Gupta-Bleuler quantization prescription which can be also formulated as conversion at the classical level. This is to say, we can omit constraint
\mathfrak{d}_{-A}
and thus arrive at the system with one octuplet of complex fermionic first class constraints

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{A}_{-}=\bar{\Pi}{}^{A}_{-}+2ip_{=}\Theta^{-A}\approx 0\;\qquad

(2.111)

generating complex gauge supersymmetry. Upon quantization, this first class fermionic constraint can be imposed on the state vector.

III
Quantization of type IIB superparticle and on-shell superfields of linearized type IIB supergravity

III.1
Quantization

The quantization procedure in the generalized coordinate representation implies the replacement of phase space variables by quantum operators and Poisson brackets by graded commutators (i.e. commutators or anti-commutators),

[...,...\}_{PB}\quad\mapsto\quad\frac{1}{i}\,[...,...\}\;.\qquad

(3.1)

In the coordinate representation this prescription is realized by replacing the canonical momenta by derivatives,

\displaystyle p_{=}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle-i\partial_{=}\equiv-i\frac{\partial}{\partial X^{=}}\;,\qquad\Pi_{-A}\mapsto-i\partial_{-A}\equiv-i\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta^{-A}}\;,\qquad\bar{\Pi}{}^{A}_{-}\mapsto-i\bar{\partial}{}^{A}_{-}\equiv-i\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}}\;,\qquad

(3.2)

and covariant momenta by covariant derivatives
(
2.98
), (
A.26
)–(
A.29
), (
2.97
)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle-iD^{(0)}\;,\qquad\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{ij}\mapsto-i\tilde{D}{}^{ij}=-i\left(D^{ij}-\frac{1}{2}(w^{A}\gamma^{ij})_{q}\partial_{qA}-\frac{1}{2}(\bar{w}_{A}\gamma^{ij})_{q}\bar{\partial}_{q}^{A}\right)\;,\qquad

(3.4)

\displaystyle{}\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{\#i}\mapsto-iD^{\#i}\;,\qquad\mathfrak{d}^{=i}\mapsto-iD^{=i}\;,\qquad

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{A}{}^{B}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle-iD_{A}{}^{B}\;,\qquad

(3.5)

Notice that with this prescription the fermionic constraints (
2.109
) are represented by covariant fermionic derivatives in the reduced superspace

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{-A}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle-iD_{-A}\;,\qquad D_{-A}=\partial_{-A}+2i\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}\partial_{=}\;,\qquad

(3.6)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathfrak{d}}{}^{A}_{-}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle-i\bar{D}{}^{A}_{-}\;,\qquad\bar{D}{}^{A}_{-}=\bar{\partial}{}^{A}_{-}+2i\Theta^{-A}\partial_{=}\;.\qquad

(3.7)

These covariant derivatives obey the
SU(8)
supersymmetry algebra

\displaystyle\{D_{-A},D_{-A}\}=0\;,\qquad\{D_{-A},\bar{D}{}^{B}_{-}\}=+4i\partial_{=}\delta_{A}{}^{B}\;,\qquad\{\bar{D}{}^{A}_{-}\,,\,\bar{D}{}^{B}_{-}\}=0\;.\qquad

(3.8)

In the reduced phase space and after conversion of fermionic constraints, we have the system with only the first class constraints, bosonic (
2.105
)– (
2.108
) and fermionic (
2.111
). According to the Dirac prescription
Dirac:1963
, the quantum version of these constraints have to be imposed on the state vector. Thus our state vector superfield

\Xi=\Xi(x^{=},v,w;\Theta^{-A},\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A})

(3.9)

should satisfy the following set of equations

\displaystyle\left(D^{(0)}-2X^{=}\partial_{=}-\Theta^{-A}\partial_{-A}-\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}\bar{\partial}{}^{A}_{-}\right)\Xi=0\;,\qquad

(3.10)

\displaystyle\tilde{D}{}^{ij}\Xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(D^{ij}+\frac{1}{2}\left((w^{A}\gamma^{ij})_{q}\partial_{qA}+(\bar{w}_{A}\gamma^{ij})_{q}\bar{\partial}_{q}^{A}\right)\right)\Xi=0\;,\qquad

(3.11)

\displaystyle D^{=i}\Xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,\qquad

(3.12)

\displaystyle\left(D_{B}{}^{A}+\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{B}\bar{\partial}{}^{A}_{-}-\Theta^{-A}\partial_{-B}-\frac{1}{8}\left(\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{C}\bar{\partial}{}^{C}_{-}-\Theta^{-C}\partial_{-C}\right)\right)\Xi=0\;,\qquad

(3.13)

and

\displaystyle\bar{D}{}^{A}_{-}\Xi=\left(\bar{\partial}{}^{A}_{-}+2i\Theta^{-A}\partial_{=}\right)\Xi=0\;.\qquad

(3.14)

The constraints (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) imply that the state vector superfield should be invariant under
SO(1,1)
and
SO(8)
symmetry which act, in the first case on the Lorentz harmonics and

X^{=}
,
\Theta^{-A}
and
\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}
coordinates, while in the second case on the Lorentz harmonics and
the auxiliary bridge variables

w_{q}^{A}
and
\bar{w}_{qA}
. Eq. (
3.12
) implies that
\Xi
should depend only on

v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
, but not on
v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}
Lorentz harmonic coordinate.

Eq. (
3.13
) implies that the state vector superfield is invariant under
SU(8)
transformations acting on
bosonic
w_{q}^{A}
and
\bar{w}_{qA}
and fermionic
\Theta^{-A}
and
\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{B}
coordinates. This can be used to reproduce the value of superfield at arbitrary
w_{q}^{A}
and
\bar{w}_{qA}
starting from its ’value’ at some fixed point of
SU(8)
group manifold, e.g. at
w_{q}^{A}=\delta_{q}^{A}=\bar{w}_{qA}
. We shall turn to the
discussion on this point later.

Finally, (
3.14
) is the chirality condition imposed on our state vector superfield. It is solved by

\displaystyle\Xi(x^{=},v,w;\Theta^{-},\bar{\Theta}^{-})=e^{2i\Theta^{-A}\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}\partial_{=}}\Phi(x^{=},v,w;\Theta^{-})=\;\Phi(x^{=}_{L},v,w;\Theta^{-}),\qquad

(3.15)

where
x^{=}_{L}:=x^{=}+2i\Theta^{-A}\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}=e^{2i\Theta^{-A}\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}\partial_{=}}x^{=}

and superfield
\Phi(x^{=},v,w;\Theta^{-})
depends on
\Theta^{-A}
but not on its c.c.
\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}
. Hence its decomposition on fermionic variables reads

\displaystyle\Phi=\phi+\Theta^{-A}\psi^{+}_{A}+\frac{1}{2}\Theta^{-B}\Theta^{-A}\phi^{\#}_{AB}+\frac{1}{3!}\Theta^{-C}\Theta^{-B}\Theta^{-A}\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC}+\frac{1}{4!}\Theta^{-D}\Theta^{-C}\Theta^{-B}\Theta^{-A}\phi^{[+4]}_{ABCD}+

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{5!\,3!}\epsilon_{A_{1}\ldots A_{5}B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}}\Theta^{-A_{5}}\ldots\Theta^{-A_{1}}\tilde{\psi}{}^{B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}+5}+\frac{1}{6!\,2!}\epsilon_{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}B_{1}B_{2}}\Theta^{-A_{6}}\ldots\Theta^{-A_{1}}\tilde{\phi}{}^{B_{1}B_{2}+6}+\qquad

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{7!}\epsilon_{A_{1}\ldots A_{7}B}\Theta^{-A_{7}}\ldots\Theta^{-A_{1}}\tilde{\psi}{}^{B+7}+\frac{1}{8!}\epsilon_{A_{1}\ldots A_{7}B}\Theta^{-A_{8}}\ldots\Theta^{-A_{1}}\tilde{\phi}^{+8}\;.\qquad

(3.16)

where all the fields depend on
x^{=}
,
on the
Lorentz harmonics
v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}
and on the auxiliary
w
–coordinates.

Up to the type of variables the field components depend on, the above superfield is similar to the
chiral four dimensional
{\cal N}=8
superfield which can be obtained by quantization of the massless superparticle in

D=4
,
{\cal N}=8
. However, as in this case, and also in the case of light-cone description of the linearized
type IIB supergravity
Green:1982tk
;
Howe:1983sra
, a superfield that obeys only the chirality condition
does not describe irreducible representation of supersymmetry (type IIB supersymmetry in our case). Then, as it is done with the aim to obtain the
so-called on-shell superfields for D=4
{\cal N}=8
supergravity theory
Arkani-Hamed:2008gz
;
Brandhuber:2008pf
;
Elvang:2015rqa
and light-cone superfield description of linearized type IIB supergravity
Green:1982tk
;
Howe:1983sra
we have to impose additional condition to make the representation irreducible
17
17

17

See
Bandos:2025pxv
for discussion of similar problem in the quantization of type IIA D
0
-brane model.
.

We impose the following duality relations between half-maximal derivative of our state vector superfield
\Xi
and of its conjugate
\bar{\Xi}
:

\displaystyle D_{-A_{1}}\ldots D_{-A_{4}}\Xi=\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon_{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}B_{4}}\bar{D}{}_{-}^{B_{1}}\ldots\bar{D}{}_{-}^{B_{4}}\bar{\Xi}\;,\qquad(\Xi)^{*}=\bar{\Xi}\;.

(3.17)

Taking into account the chirality of the state vector superfield (
3.14
) and anti-chirality of its conjugate

\displaystyle D_{-A}\bar{\Xi}=0\;,

(3.18)

and using the algebra (
3.8
), we find that higher components of our state vector superfield are now expressed through the conjugate to the lower components
18
18

18

One should not be worried by appearance of inverse power of bosonic derivative
\partial_{=}
as, on one hand, the same happens in the light-cone approach
Green:1982tk
and, on the other hand, because the main use is for the Fourier image of this superfield with respect to
x^{=}
, in which
\partial_{=}
is replaced by
p_{=}
. Just this is the so-called on-shell superfield which can be considered as
one particle counterpart of the superamplitudes and
p_{=}
in it appears in the expression for light-like momenta
p_{a}=p_{=}1/8\,v_{q}^{\;-}\tilde{\sigma}_{a}v_{p}^{\;-}
(see (
2.28
)).

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}{}^{B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}[+5]}=\propto\frac{1}{\partial_{=}}({\psi}^{[+3]}_{B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}})^{*}\;,\qquad\tilde{\phi}{}^{B_{1}B_{2}[+6]}=\propto\frac{1}{(\partial_{=})^{2}}({\phi}_{B_{1}B_{2}}^{[+2]})^{*}\;,\qquad

(3.19)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}{}^{B[+7]}=\propto\frac{1}{(\partial_{=})^{3}}({\psi}_{B}^{[+]})^{*}\;,\qquad\tilde{\phi}^{[+8]}=\propto\frac{1}{(\partial_{=})^{4}}({\phi})^{*}\;,\qquad

(3.20)

as well as the duality relations between the intermediate component and its c.c.,

\phi^{[+4]}_{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}=\frac{1}{4!\,4!}\epsilon_{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}B_{4}}\tilde{\phi}{}^{B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}B_{4}[+4]}\;,\qquad(\phi^{[+4]}_{B_{1}\ldots B_{4}})^{*}=\tilde{\phi}{}^{B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}B_{4}}\;.

(3.21)

Let us show that the independent components of our on-shell superfields describe linearized type IIB supergravity, or, to be more precise, the solutions of type IIB supergravity equations.

III.2
On-shell superfield describes type IIB supergravity

To prove that our on-shell superfield obeying (
3.14
) and (
3.17
) describes linearized type IIB supergravity we shall follow the line of the analysis of the light–cone superfield approach by Green and Schwartz
Green:1982tk
.
We begin by restricting the consideration
to the point
w_{q}^{A}=\delta_{q}^{A}=\bar{w}_{qA}
of the
SU(8)
sector of
the
enlarged Lorentz harmonic superspace. This allows
us to use straightforwardly the properties of
SO(8)
’
\gamma
’–matrices
Green:1983hw
which we collect in Appendix
B
.

At this ’
SU(8)
point’ our state vector superfield and its complex conjugate obey

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{-}^{q}\Xi=0\;,\qquad\bar{D}_{-}^{q}=\bar{\partial}{}_{-}^{q}+2i{\Theta}^{-q}\partial_{=}\;,\qquad

(3.22)

\displaystyle D_{-q}\bar{\Xi}=0\;,\qquad D_{-q}=\partial_{-q}+2i\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{q}\partial_{=}\;,\qquad

(3.23)

where
{\Theta}^{-q}
and
\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{q}
are defined in (
2.88
),
and also

\displaystyle D_{-q_{1}}\ldots D_{-q_{4}}\Xi=\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}p_{1}\ldots p_{4}}\bar{D}^{p_{1}}_{-}\ldots\bar{D}_{-}^{p_{4}}\bar{\Xi}\;.

(3.24)

As a result, the set of independent components of our on-shell superfield includes a complex scalar and
unrestricted antisymmetric spin-tensors

\displaystyle\phi=\Xi|_{0}\;,\qquad\psi^{+}_{q}={D}_{-q}\Xi|_{0}\;,\qquad\phi^{\#}_{qp}={D}_{-q}\bar{D}_{-p}\Xi|_{0}\;,\qquad\psi^{[+3]}_{qpr}={D}_{-q}{D}_{-p}{D}_{-r}\Xi|_{0}\;,\qquad

(3.25)

as well as self-dual antisymmetric spin–tensor

\displaystyle\phi^{[+4]}_{q_{1}...q_{4}}=D_{-q_{1}}\ldots D_{-q_{4}}\Xi|_{0}=\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}p_{1}\ldots p_{4}}\bar{D}_{-}^{p_{1}}\ldots\bar{D}_{-}^{p_{4}}\bar{\Xi}|_{0}=\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}p_{1}\ldots p_{4}}(\phi^{[+4]}_{p_{1}...p_{4}})^{*}\;.

(3.26)

Here and below
|_{0}
denotes the leading component of superfield i.e. its ’value’ at
\Theta^{-q}=0=\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{q}
.

III.2.1
Bosonic fields of type IIB supergravity from spin-tensors

The bosonic sector of type IIB supergravity contains i) an axion and
a
dilaton, which can be collected in one complex scalar, ii)
a
complex 2-form field containing NS-NS and RR sector 2-forms, ii)
a
self-dual antisymmetric 4-form gauge field (from RR sector) and iv) graviton. Their linearized equations can be solved, respectively, in terms of i) a
complex scalar depending on the light-like momentum, which in our case can be associated with the
leading component of our complex state vector superfield
\phi(x^{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})
, ii) a complex antisymmetric
SO(8)
tensor field

c^{ij}=-c^{ji}=c^{[ij]}
, iii) a real antisymmetric self-dual 4-th rank SO(8) tensor
c^{ijkl}=\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{ijkli^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}c^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}
and iv) a symmetric traceless second rank tensor
h^{ij}=h^{ji}
,
h^{ii}=0
.
All these fields should depend on
(x^{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})
, transform covariantly under
SO(8)
symmery, and should be inert under
SO(1,1)
.
We however will not concentrate on this latter property as the weight of
the
fields with respect to
SO(1,1)
can be easily compensated by corresponding powers of
\partial_{=}
in
x^{=}
–coordinate representation or by
powers of
p_{=}
when passing to the Fourier images of the fields.

Let us begin from the 2-nd rank antisymmetric spin-tensor
\phi_{qp}^{\#}
. Using the identity

\delta_{q[r}\delta_{s]p}=-\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}\gamma^{ij}_{rs}

(Eq. (
B.6
) in Appendix
B
), we find that

\phi_{qp}^{\#}=-\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}\,\gamma^{ij}_{rs}\,\phi_{rs}^{\#}=\gamma^{ij}_{qp}c^{ij}\;,\qquad c^{ij}=-\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}\phi_{qp}^{\#}\;.

(3.27)

Thus the complex antisymmetric spin-tensor component of our state vector superfield is in one-to one correspondence with the complex antisymmetric
SO(8)
tensor
c^{ij}(x^{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})
describing the solution of free equations for complex 2-form field
c_{\mu\nu}(x^{\rho})
,
\partial^{\mu}\partial_{[\mu}c_{\nu\rho]}=0
, after the suitable gauge fixing.

To be more precise, as
c^{ij}
should be invariant under
SO(1,1)
gauge symmetry acting on the Lorentz harmonics, the r.h.s. of (
3.27
) should contain
operator
(\partial_{=})^{-1}
or, after Fourier transform with respect to
x^{=}
, multiplier

p_{=}=\rho^{\#}
. In this section we, however, conventionally omit such multipliers to simplify and to make more transparent the structure of (
3.27
) as well as of Eqs. (
3.36
)–(
3.37
) and (
3.39
) below.

Let us turn to the complex antisymmetric 4-rank
SO(8)
spin-tensor component of our state vector superfield, which is related to its c.c. by the duality relation (
3.26
). First we use the same identity (
B.6
) to find

\displaystyle\phi^{[+4]}_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}}=\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ij}_{[q_{1}q_{2}}\gamma^{kl}_{q_{3}q_{4}]}\phi^{ij,kl}\;,\qquad\phi^{ij,kl}=\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ij}_{[q_{1}q_{2}}\gamma^{kl}_{q_{3}q_{4}]}\phi^{[+4]}_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}}\;.

(3.28)

Now we
decompose

\gamma^{ij}_{[q_{1}q_{2}}\gamma^{kl}_{q_{3}q_{4}]}
onto irreducible parts (see (
B.25
), (
B.16
), (
B.15
) in Appendix
B
)

\displaystyle\gamma^{ij}_{[qp}\gamma^{kl}_{rs]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t^{ijkl}_{qprs}-\frac{2}{3}\delta^{[i|[k}t^{l]|j]}_{qprs}\;,\qquad

(3.29)

\displaystyle t^{ijkl}_{qprs}:=\gamma^{[ij}_{[qp}\gamma^{kl]}_{rs]}\;,\qquad t^{ij}_{qprs}:=\gamma^{ik}_{[qp}\gamma^{jk}_{rs]}=t^{ji}_{qprs}\;\qquad

(3.30)

which are, respectively totally antisymmetric and anti–self–dual in both quadruplets of indices,

\displaystyle t^{ijkl}_{qprs}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{qprsq^{\prime}p^{\prime}r^{\prime}s^{\prime}}t^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}_{q^{\prime}p^{\prime}r^{\prime}s^{\prime}}=-\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{ijkli^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}t^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}_{q^{\prime}p^{\prime}r^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\;,\qquad

(3.31)

and symmetric traceless on vector indices while self-dual on quadruplet of spin-tensor indices,

\displaystyle t^{ij}_{qprs}=t^{ji}_{qprs}\,,\qquad t^{ii}_{qprs}=0\;,\qquad t^{ij}_{qprs}=\;\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{qprsq^{\prime}p^{\prime}r^{\prime}s^{\prime}}t^{ij}_{q^{\prime}p^{\prime}r^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\;\qquad

(3.32)

(see Appendix
B
).

Using (
3.29
) and the above properties we find

\displaystyle\phi^{ij,kl}=ic^{ijkl}-\frac{2}{3}\delta^{[i|[k}h^{l]|j]}\;,\qquad

(3.33)

\displaystyle h^{ij}=\phi^{ik,jk}=\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ik}_{[q_{1}q_{2}}\gamma^{jk}_{q_{3}q_{4}]}\phi^{[+4]}_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}}

\displaystyle\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad h^{ij}=h^{ji}\;,\qquad h^{ii}=0\;,\;

(3.34)

\displaystyle ic^{ijkl}=\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{[ij}_{[q_{1}q_{2}}\gamma^{kl]}_{q_{3}q_{4}]}\phi^{[+4]}_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}}=-\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon_{ijkli^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}\;ic^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}\;.

(3.35)

Eqs. (
3.28
) and (
3.33
) imply

\displaystyle\phi^{[+4]}_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}}=\frac{1}{16}\left(ic^{ijkl}t^{ijkl}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}}+\frac{2}{3}t^{ij}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}}h^{ij}\right)\;

(3.36)

and
(\phi^{[+4]}_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}})^{*}=\frac{1}{16}\left(-i(c^{ijkl})^{*}t^{ijkl}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}}+\frac{2}{3}t^{ij}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}}(h^{ij})^{*}\right)\;

so that, due to (
3.32
) and (
3.31
),
