\displaystyle\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}p_{1}\ldots p_{4}}(\phi^{[+4]}_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}})^{*}=\frac{1}{256}\left(i(c^{ijkl})^{*}t^{ijkl}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}}+\frac{2}{3}t^{ij}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}q_{4}}(h^{ij})^{*}\right)\;

(3.37)

and hence (
3.24
) implies the reality of both fields,

(c^{ijkl})^{*}=c^{ijkl}\;,\qquad(h^{ij})^{*}=h^{ij}\;.\qquad

(3.38)

Thus we have shown that the bosonic components of the state vector superfield describe the bosonic fields of type IIB supergravity multiplet.

III.2.2
Gravitino and dilatino of type IIB supergravity from fermionic spin tensors

Let us turn to the fermionic components of our state vector superfield, complex s–spinor
\psi^{+}_{q}=D_{-q}\Xi|_{0}
and complex spin-tensor
\psi^{[+3]}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}}=D_{-q_{1}}D_{-q_{2}}D_{-q_{3}}\Xi|_{0}
. The first one,

\psi^{+}_{q}(x^{=},v^{-})
(or, more precisely
its Fourier image
\psi^{+}_{q}(p_{=},v^{-})=\int dx^{=}e^{-ip_{=}x^{=}}\psi^{+}_{q}(x^{=},v^{-})
)
clearly describes the complex solution
\chi_{\alpha}=(\chi_{\alpha}^{1}+i\chi_{\alpha}^{2})=v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\psi^{+}_{q}(p_{=},v^{-})
of the
10D
Weyl equation in momentum representation,

p_{\mu}\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu\alpha\beta}\chi_{\beta}=0
with light-like momentum composed from spinor harmonics as in (
2.28
). Thus
\psi^{+}_{q}(x^{=},v^{-})
describes dilatini of type IIB supergravity.

To clarify the meaning of the complex fermionic fields
\psi_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}}
, we observe
that it can be expressed in terms of
\gamma
- traceless SO(8) vector–spinor field
\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}
by

\psi^{[+3]}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}}=-\frac{1}{8}t^{i}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}\dot{q}}\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}=\frac{1}{8}\gamma^{ij}_{[q_{1}q_{2}}\gamma^{j}_{q_{3}]\dot{q}}\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}\;.

(3.39)

To be convinced
that the r.h.s. gives the general structure of an antisymmetric spin-tensor of rank 3, one can notice
that the number of independent components in the expression on the l.h.s
is
\left\{\begin{matrix}3\cr 8\end{matrix}\right\}=56
, and the same is the number of components of gamma-traceless vector-tensor
\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}
,
64-8=56
. One may note that the r.h.s. of (
3.39
) can include an arbitrary vector spinor
\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}
with 64 components. However,
substituting for
\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}
the expression
\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}+\chi_{p}\gamma^{i}_{p\dot{q}}
in the r.h.s. of (
3.39
) does not change it. Then equivalence relation
\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}\approx\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}+\chi_{p}\gamma^{i}_{p\dot{q}}
can be treated as a counterpart of gauge symmetry and can be used
to impose
8
conditions

\gamma^{i}_{p\dot{q}}\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}=0

(3.40)

thus equating the number of degrees of freedom in the l.h.s. and r.h.s. of (
3.39
).

A more explicit proof of one-to one correspondence consists in using
(
B.26
) from Appendix
B
to solve Eq. (
3.39
) by

\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}=\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}-\frac{1}{8}(\tilde{\gamma}{}^{i}{\gamma}{}^{j}\psi^{j})_{\dot{q}}=-\frac{1}{6}\psi_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}}t^{i}_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}\dot{q}}=\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{ij}_{[q_{1}q_{2}}\gamma^{j}_{q_{3}]\dot{q}}\psi_{q_{1}q_{2}q_{3}}\;,

(3.41)

where the first equality is written with the use of (
3.40
), while the second is valid also for unrestricted
\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}
(see also (
B.27
)).

Thus we have shown that the fermionic components of our state vector superfield are in one-to one correspondence with the solutions of dilatini and gravitini equations of linearized type IIB supergravity. This completes the proof that our state vector superfield describes linearized type IIB supergravity.

III.2.3
SU(8) symmetric form of correspondence with type IIB supergravity fields

Although we have derived the above results at a point
\bar{w}_{qA}=\delta_{qA}={w}_{q}^{A}
of SU(8) sector of the extended Lorentz harmonic superspace, it can be lifted to the SU(8) covariant expressions. To this end let us
introduce the following
SO(8)
tensors carrying
SU(8)
indices

\displaystyle\gamma^{ij}_{AB}=\bar{w}_{qA}\bar{w}_{pB}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}\;,\qquad\bar{\gamma}^{ij\;AB}={w}_{q}^{A}{w}_{p}^{B}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}=(\gamma^{ij}_{AB})^{*}\;,\qquad

(3.42)

(see also (
A.40
)) as well as (
cf.

Bandos:2025pxv
)

\displaystyle t^{ijkl}_{ABCD}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma^{[ij}_{[AB}\gamma^{kl]}_{CD]}=\bar{w}_{qA}\bar{w}_{pB}\bar{w}_{rC}\bar{w}_{sD}\gamma^{ik}_{[qp}\gamma^{jk}_{rs]}=(\bar{t}^{ijkl\,ABCD})^{*}\;,\qquad

(3.43)

\displaystyle t^{ij}_{ABCD}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gamma^{ik}_{[AB}\gamma^{jk}_{CD]}=\bar{w}_{qA}\bar{w}_{pB}\bar{w}_{rC}\bar{w}_{sD}\gamma^{ik}_{[qp}\gamma^{jk}_{rs]}=\;(\bar{t}^{ij\,ABCD})^{*}\;.\qquad

(3.44)

Then it is easy to observe that Eqs. (
3.27
), (
3.36
) and their c.c. can be obtained as ”values” at the point
\bar{w}_{qA}=\delta_{qA}=w_{q}^{A}
of the
SU(8)
sector of the enlarged Lorentz harmonic superspace of the following expressions for the components of the state vector superfield

\displaystyle\phi_{AB}^{\#}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ij}_{AB}c^{ij}\;,\qquad c^{ij}=-\frac{1}{16}\bar{\gamma}^{ijAB}\phi_{AB}^{\#}\;,

(3.45)

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}^{\#AB}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16}\bar{\gamma}^{ijAB}c^{ij*}=(\phi_{AB}^{\#})^{*}\;,\qquad c^{ij*}=-\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ij}_{AB}\bar{\phi}^{\#AB}\;,

(3.46)

\displaystyle\phi^{[+4]}_{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}\left(ic^{ijkl}t^{ijkl}_{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}A_{4}}+\frac{2}{3}t^{ij}_{A_{1}A_{2}A_{3}A_{4}}h^{ij}\right)\;

(3.47)

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}{}^{[+4]A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\phi^{[+4]}_{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}})^{*}=\frac{1}{16}\left(-ic^{ijkl}\bar{t}^{ijkl\,ABCD}+\frac{2}{3}\bar{t}^{ij\,ABCD}h^{ij}\right)\;.

(3.48)

Notice that the last two fields
obey (
3.26
) due to the following identities

\displaystyle\bar{t}{}^{ijkl\,ABCD}=-\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{ABCDEFGH}t^{ijkl}_{EFGH}=-\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{ijkli^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}\bar{t}{}^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}\,ABCD}=(t^{ijkl}_{ABCD})^{*}\;,\qquad

(3.49)

\displaystyle\bar{t}{}^{ij\;ABCD}=+\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{ABCDEFGH}t^{ij}_{EFGH}=(t^{ij}_{ABCD})^{*}\;\qquad

(3.50)

which follow from (
3.31
), (
3.32
) and

{\rm det}(w_{q}^{A})=1
(see (
2.91
)).

The generic fermionic field components of the state vector superfield are
\psi^{+}_{A}
and
\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC}=\psi^{+}_{[ABC]}
. The first of these complex fields and its c.c. are related to
\psi^{+}_{q}
and
\bar{\psi}^{+q}
by

\psi^{+}_{A}=\bar{{\rm w}}_{qA}\psi^{+}_{q}\;,\qquad\bar{\psi}^{+A}={\rm w}_{q}^{A}(\psi^{+}_{q})^{*}\;.\qquad

(3.51)

To deal with
\psi^{[+3]}_{[ABC]}
and its c.c., we need to introduce a companion of our bridge variables
({\rm w}_{q}{}^{A}\in SU(8)
(
2.91
),
{w}_{\dot{q}}{}^{A}=(\bar{{\rm w}}_{\dot{q}A})^{*}\in SU(8)
, which connect fundamental and anti-fundamental representations of
SU(8)
with c-spinor representation of
SO(8)
,

\displaystyle{\rm w}_{\dot{q}}{}^{A}\bar{{\rm w}}_{\dot{p}A}=\delta_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}\;,\qquad\bar{{\rm w}}_{\dot{q}A}{\rm w}_{\dot{q}}{}^{B}=\delta_{A}{}^{B}\;,\qquad{\rm det}({\rm w}_{\dot{q}}{}^{A})=1\;,\qquad

(3.52)

This allows us to define

\displaystyle\gamma^{i}_{AB}=\bar{{\rm w}}_{{q}A}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\bar{{\rm w}}_{\dot{p}B}=:\tilde{\gamma}{}^{i}_{BA}\;,\qquad\tilde{\bar{\gamma}}{}^{i\,BA}:=\bar{\gamma}{}^{i\,AB}={\rm w}_{q}^{A}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}{\rm w}_{\dot{p}}^{B}\;\qquad

(3.53)

which obey

\displaystyle\gamma^{i}_{AC}\tilde{\bar{\gamma}}{}^{j\,CB}=\delta^{ij}\delta_{A}{}^{B}+\gamma^{ij}{}_{A}{}^{B}\;,\qquad\gamma^{ij}{}_{A}{}^{B}=\bar{{\rm w}}_{{q}A}\gamma^{ij}_{qp}{\rm w}_{p}{}^{B}=-(\gamma^{ij}{}_{B}{}^{A})^{*}\;.

(3.54)

Now we can check that Eq. (
3.39
) and its c.c. can be obtained at the point
{\rm w}_{q}^{A}=\delta_{q}^{A}
,
{\rm w}_{\dot{q}}^{A}=\delta_{\dot{q}}^{A}
of the SU(8) sector of the enlarged Lorentz harmonic superspace from the following equations

\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC}=\frac{1}{8}\gamma^{ij}_{[AB}\gamma^{j}_{C]D}\psi^{iD}\;,\qquad\bar{\psi}^{[+3]ABC}=(\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC})^{*}=\frac{1}{8}\bar{\gamma}^{ij[AB}\gamma^{C]Dj}\bar{\psi}^{i}_{D}\;.

(3.55)

with

\psi^{iD}=\psi^{i}_{\dot{q}}w_{\dot{q}}^{D}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bar{\psi}^{i}_{D}=(\psi^{iD})^{*}=\bar{\psi}^{i}_{\dot{q}}\bar{w}_{\dot{q}D}

(3.56)

obeying

\displaystyle\gamma^{i}_{AB}\psi^{iB}=0=\bar{\gamma}{}^{iAB}\bar{\psi}^{i}_{B}\;.

(3.57)

The inverse relations read

\displaystyle\psi^{iD}=\frac{1}{6}\bar{\gamma}{}^{ij[AB}\bar{\gamma}{}^{C]Dj}\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC}\;,\qquad\bar{\psi}{}^{i}_{D}=\frac{1}{6}\gamma^{ij}_{[AB}\gamma^{j}_{C]D}\bar{\psi}{}^{[+3]ABC}\;,\qquad

(3.58)

and, with (
3.55
), reproduce (
3.41
) at the point

{\rm w}_{q}^{A}=\delta_{q}^{A}
,
{\rm w}_{\dot{q}}^{A}=\delta_{\dot{q}}^{A}
of the SU(8) sector of the enlarged Lorentz harmonic superspace
19
19

19

Eq. (
3.57
) is obeyed by (
3.58
) due to the identity

\gamma^{ij}_{[AB}\gamma^{ij}_{C]}{}^{D}=0
following from
\gamma^{ij}_{[qp}\gamma^{ij}_{r]s}=0
(which in its turn follows from (
B.6
)).

. Of course, at that point the
SU(8)
symmetry is not seen as
\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}
are invariants of
SO(8)
group only.

The SU(8) transformations that act
on
{\rm w}_{q}^{A}
,
{\rm w}_{\dot{q}}^{A}

can be used to connect any ’point’ of the
SU(8)
sector of
the
enlarged Lorentz harmonic superspace to
{\rm w}_{q}^{A}=\delta_{q}^{A}
,
{\rm w}_{\dot{q}}^{A}=\delta_{\dot{q}}^{A}
’point’, so that
in the superparticle model
{\rm w}_{q}^{A}(\tau)
,
{\rm w}_{\dot{q}}^{A}(\tau)
are
Stückelberg fields
.
After quantization, these Stückelberg fields describe the complex structure used in our quantization prescription and thus the possible complex structures are in one-to-one correspondence with the points of
SU(8)/SO(8)
coset. The hidden
SU(8)
symmetry of the linearized type IIB supergravity is thus seen in the constructions which are independent on the choice of this complex structure. These include the on-shell superfield (
III.1
) and its antisymmetric spin tensor components, but not their decompositions in terms of more ”standard” fields
(
3.45
), (
3.47
), (
3.55
).
The indication of the relevance of this
SU(8)
symmetry is that the superamplitudes of type IIB supergravity should be given by multiparticle generalization of these on-shell superfields,
as it is observed in the case of
4D
supergravity

Brandhuber:2008pf
;
Arkani-Hamed:2008gz
;
Elvang:2015rqa
and
11D
supergravity
Bandos:2017eof
;
Bandos:2017zap
.

As type IIB and type IIA supergravity are related by T-duality, we should expect this
SU(8)
symmetry to be seen also in the linearized type IIA supergravity as described by quantization of the type IIA superparticle in its spinor moving frame formulation. We perform such a quantization in the next section.

IV
Type IIA superparticle quantization and hidden SU(8) symmetry of linearized IIA supergravity

The action of type IIA massless superparticle in its spinor moving frame formulation can be written in the form of Eq. (
2.29
),

\displaystyle S^{IIA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\int d\tau\rho^{\#}\,E^{a}_{\tau}\,v^{-}_{q}\tilde{\sigma}_{a}v^{-}_{q}\quad=\quad\int\limits_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\,E^{a}\,u_{a}^{=}\quad=\int\limits_{{\cal W}^{1}}\rho^{\#}\,E^{=}\;,\qquad

(4.1)

but with pull–back
E^{a}=d\tau E_{\tau}^{a}
of the bosonic type IIA Volkov-Akulov 1–form, the bosonic forms of the supervielbein of flat type IIA superspace

\displaystyle E^{a}=\Pi^{a}=dx^{a}-id\theta^{1}\sigma^{a}\theta^{1}-id\theta^{2}\tilde{\sigma}{}^{a}\theta^{2}\;,\qquad E^{\alpha 1}=d\theta^{\alpha 1}\;,\qquad E_{\alpha}^{2}=d\theta_{\alpha}^{2}\;.\qquad

(4.2)

This differential forms are, and hence the action (
4.1
) is, invariant under type IIA supersymmetry which acts on the coordinate functions by

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}x^{a}=i\theta^{1}\sigma^{a}\varepsilon^{1}+i\theta^{2}\tilde{\sigma}{}^{a}\varepsilon^{2}\;,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta^{\alpha 1}=\varepsilon^{\alpha 1}\;,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta_{\alpha}^{2}=\varepsilon_{\alpha}^{2}\;\qquad\;

(4.3)

and leaves
the spinor moving frame variables and
the field
\rho^{\#}
intact.

This dynamical system can be considered as particle moving in the type IIA Lorentz harmonic superspace (
cf.
(
2.11
)

\Sigma^{(10+16|16+16)}_{IIA}\;=\;\{{\cal Z}^{{\mathfrak{M}}}_{IIA}\}=\{(x^{a},\theta^{\alpha 1},\theta_{\alpha}^{2};(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+})\}\;

(4.4)

which contains the same Lorentz harmonic sector
[SO(1,9)/SO(1,1)\times SO(8)]
as IIB Lorentz harmonic superspace (
2.11
)
(see discussion and comments on the notation around Eq. (
2.11
)).
In the central coordinate basis
\{{\cal Z}^{{\mathfrak{M}}}_{IIA}\}
, the only difference with type IIB case is the chirality
of the second fermionic coordinate reflected by the position of its Majorana-Weyl (MW) spinor index (
\theta_{\alpha}^{2}

vs

\theta^{\alpha 2}
in type IIB case) which is now different from the chirality of the first fermionic coordinate
\theta^{\alpha 1}
.

One can not rise or lower
the
10D
MW spinor indices
in an
SO(1,9)
covariant manner which indicates that to describe
T-duality relating the flat type IIA and type IIB superspaces we have
to break the Lorentz covariance.
If we single-out some direction in the bosonic sector of type II superspace by choosing a constant vector
k_{a}
, we can use it to write the relation between type IIB and type IIA coordinates

\theta_{\alpha}^{2}=k^{a}\sigma_{a\alpha\beta}\theta^{\beta 2}_{IIB}\;.

(4.5)

This is invertible,

\theta^{\alpha 2}_{IIB}=\frac{1}{k_{a}k^{a}}k_{b}\tilde{\sigma}{}^{b\alpha\beta}\theta_{\beta}^{2}
if the vector is not light-like,
k_{a}k^{a}\not=0
. Actually the standard approach to T-duality is to chose the
k_{a}
to be spacelike
k_{a}k^{a}<0
, however timelike T-duality has been discussed since the late 90s

Hull:1998vg
.

As the chirality of two fermionic supervielbein forms of type IIA superspace (
4.2
), in the split form of the pull–backs of type IIA flat supervielbein

\displaystyle E^{=}=E^{a}u_{a}^{=}\;,\qquad E^{\#}=E^{a}u_{a}^{\#}\;,\qquad E^{i}=E^{a}u_{a}^{i}\;,\qquad

(4.6)

\displaystyle E^{-q1}=d\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\;,\qquad E^{+\dot{q}1}=d\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad

(4.7)

\displaystyle E^{-\dot{q}2}=d\theta_{\alpha}^{2}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad E^{+q2}=d\theta_{\alpha}^{2}v^{+\alpha}_{q}\;\qquad

(4.8)

the components of two fermionic
1
–forms with the same
SO(1,1)
weight carry indices of different (s-spinor and c-spinor) representation of
SO(8)
group (
cf.
(
2.30
), (
2.31
))
20
20

20

Of course, this can be related by triality using also
SO(8)
vector (v-) representation, in particular

k^{i}=k_{\mu}u^{\mu i}
(see below).
. This is what makes the quantization of type IIA superparticle a bit different from type IIB case.

IV.1
Type IIA superparticle action in analytical coordinate basis

The detailed discussion of type IIB case in sec.
II
allows us to proceed much faster and, in particular, to pass directly
to the study of type IIA superparticle in the analytical basis of Lorentz harmonic superspace

\displaystyle\Sigma^{(10+16|16+16)}_{IIA}\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\{{\cal Z}^{{\mathfrak{M}}}_{An\,IIA}\}=\{(X^{=},X^{\#},X^{i}_{An}\;;\theta^{-1}_{q},\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\;,\theta^{+1}_{\dot{q}},\theta^{+2}_{q}\;,(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}))\}\;,

(4.9)

\displaystyle X^{=}=x^{a}u_{a}^{=}\;,\qquad X^{\#}=x^{a}u_{a}^{\#}\;,\qquad

(4.10)

\displaystyle X^{i}_{An}=x^{a}u_{a}^{i}+i\theta^{-1}_{q}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\theta^{+1}_{\dot{p}}+i\theta^{+2}_{q}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\theta^{-2}_{\dot{p}}\;,\qquad

(4.11)

\displaystyle\theta^{-1}_{q}=\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\;,\qquad\theta^{+1}_{\dot{q}}=\theta^{\alpha 1}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad

(4.12)

\displaystyle\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}=\theta_{\alpha}^{2}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\theta^{+2}_{q}=\theta_{\alpha}^{2}v^{+\alpha}_{q}\;.\qquad

(4.13)

The action (
4.1
) in this coordinate basis reads

\displaystyle S^{IIA}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{{\cal W}^{1}}{\cal L}_{1}^{IIA}\;,\qquad{\cal L}_{1}^{IIA}=\rho^{\#}E^{=}=\rho^{\#}\left(DX^{=}-2iD\theta^{-1}_{q}\,\theta^{-1}_{q}-2iD\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\,\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}-\Omega^{=i}X_{An}^{i}\right)\;,\qquad

(4.14)

where the covariant derivatives are defined with the use of the composite
SO(1,1)
and
SO(8)
connection given by Cartan forms
\Omega^{0}
and
\Omega^{ij}
, Eq. (
A.6
),
(
cf.
(
2.52
)–(
2.54
))

\displaystyle DX^{=}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle dX^{=}+2X^{=}\Omega^{(0)}\;,\qquad

(4.15)

\displaystyle D\theta^{-1}_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\theta^{-1}_{q}+\Omega^{(0)}\theta^{-1}_{q}+{1\over 4}\Omega^{ij}\theta^{-1}_{p}\gamma_{{p}{q}}^{ij}\;,\qquad

(4.16)

\displaystyle D\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}+\Omega^{(0)}\theta^{-2}_{q}+{1\over 4}\Omega^{ij}\theta^{-2}_{\dot{p}}\tilde{\gamma}^{ij}_{\dot{p}\dot{q}}\;.\qquad

(4.17)

The action (
4.14
) involves only coordinate functions from the sub-superspace
\Sigma^{(9+16|8+8)}\subset\Sigma^{(10+16|16+16)}
,

\Sigma^{(9+16|8+8)}=\{{\mathfrak{Z}}_{IIA}^{{\cal M}}\}=\{(X^{=},X^{i}_{An}\;;\theta^{-1}_{q},\theta^{-2}_{\dot{p}}\;,\;(v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\,v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}))\}\;

(4.18)

which is invariant under the target superspace supersymmetry (
4.3
) realized
on its coordinates as follows

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}X^{=}=2i\theta^{-1}_{q}\varepsilon^{-1}_{q}+2i\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\varepsilon^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}X^{i}_{An}=2i\theta^{-1}_{q}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\varepsilon^{+1}_{\dot{q}}-2i\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\tilde{\gamma}^{i}_{\dot{q}p}\varepsilon^{+2}_{p}\;,\qquad

(4.19)

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta^{-1}_{q}=\varepsilon^{-1}_{q}\;,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}=\varepsilon^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad

(4.20)

\displaystyle\delta_{\varepsilon}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}=0\,,\qquad\delta_{\varepsilon}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}=0\,,\qquad

(4.21)

\displaystyle\text{where}\qquad\varepsilon^{1-}_{q}=\varepsilon^{\alpha}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\,,\qquad\varepsilon^{1+}_{\dot{q}}=\varepsilon^{\alpha}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad\varepsilon^{-2}_{\dot{p}}=\varepsilon^{2}_{\alpha}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{p}}\;,\qquad\varepsilon^{+2}_{q}=\varepsilon^{2}_{\alpha}v^{+\alpha}_{q}\;.\qquad

The Hamiltonian formalism constructed in this analytical basis differs with the one we have described for IIB case only in Grassmann sector:

\displaystyle\theta^{-2}_{q}{}_{(IIB)}\mapsto\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\Pi^{2}_{-q}{}_{(IIB)}\mapsto\Pi^{2}_{-\dot{q}}\;.\qquad

(4.22)

This implies the change of the SO(8) representation of the second fermionic constraint (
2.81
) which now reads

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}^{2}_{-\dot{q}}=\Pi^{2}_{-\dot{q}}+2ip_{=}\theta^{-2}_{\dot{q}}\approx 0\;.\qquad

(4.23)

so that there is no an
SO(8)
covariant way to pass to their complex combination as in (
2.90
), neither
SO(8)
covariant way of passing to complex coordinates, as in (
2.88
) exists.

The algebra of the fermionic constraints of type IIA superparticle is

\displaystyle\{\mathfrak{d}^{1}_{-q},\mathfrak{d}^{1}_{-p}\}_{PB}=-4ip_{=}\delta_{qp}\;,\qquad\{\mathfrak{d}^{1}_{-q},\mathfrak{d}^{2}_{-\dot{q}}\}_{PB}=0\;,\qquad\{\mathfrak{d}^{2}_{-\dot{q}},\mathfrak{d}^{2}_{-\dot{p}}\}_{PB}=-4ip_{=}\delta_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}\;.\qquad

(4.24)

IV.2
Introducing the complex structure in the type IIA superparticle mechanics

To follow the way towards quantization of type IIA superparticle similar to that we have used for its type IIB cousin, we need to introduce a complex structure and split the set of real fermionic coordinates onto a pair of complex conjugate coordinates.

A possibility is to use to this end the
SO(8)/[SO(6)\times U(1)]
harmonics which were introduced for the description of
10D
SYM amplitudes in
Bandos:2017eof
. Their introduction provides a covariant counterpart of the simplest construction which explicitly breaks
SO(8)
(gauge) symmetry down to its

SO(6)\times U(1)\approx SU(4)\times U(1)\approx U(4)
subgroup.
However, in type IIA case a more natural way to introduce complex structure, both in sector of bosonic spinor variables and for Grassmann coordinates, corresponds to a covariant counterpart of breaking of
SO(8)
symmetry down to its
SO(7)
subgroup. Then, as we will see, the
SO(7)
symmetry can be enhanced till
SU(8)
in the manner similar (but not identical) to how it happens in the case of quantization of D
0
–brane performed in
Bandos:2025pxv
.

The
SO(7)
subgroup of
SO(8)
can be defined by the conditions of preservation of some constant
SO(8)

8
–vector
k^{i}
. Then, choosing this vector to be of unit length, we can construct the
SO(8)
valued
8
matrix

\displaystyle k\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{p}}:=k^{i}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}=:\tilde{k}\!\!\!/{}_{\dot{p}q}\;,\qquad k^{i}k^{i}=1\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad k\!\!\!/{}\tilde{k}\!\!\!/{}={\hbox{\mybb I}}_{8\times 8}\,\qquad

(4.25)

which can be used to relate s- and c- spinor representations for the transformations of
SO(7)\in SO(8)
. Then the conjugate complex octuplets of fermionic coordinates can be constructed as

\displaystyle\Theta^{-q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\theta^{1-}_{q}+ik\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{p}}\theta^{2-}_{\dot{q}})\;,\qquad\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\theta^{1-}_{q}-ik\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{p}}\theta^{2-}_{\dot{q}})\;.\qquad

(4.26)

Althought these complex coordinates carry
SO(8)
s-spinor indices, their construction remains invariant under such
SO(7)
subgroup of
SO(8)
which leaves invariant the
SO(8)
vector
k^{i}
. The
SO(8)/SO(7)
transformation can be used to rotate
k^{i}
and thus describe changes of complex structure defined by (
4.26
).

Notice that in Eqs. (
4.26
) we have used upper and lower s-spinor indices (
\Theta^{-q}
vs.
\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{q}=(\Theta^{-q})^{*}
), thus suggesting
SO(7)
symmetry enhancement till
SU(8)
.

Such an enhancement of
SO(7)
symmetry to
SU(8)
can be reached by introducing the
SU(8)
valued matrix variables

{w}_{q}^{A}=(\bar{{w}}_{qA})^{*}
which serve as a bridge between
SU(8)
and
SO(7)
group transformations. These obey the constraints (
2.91
)

\bar{{w}}_{qA}{w}_{q}^{B}=\delta_{A}{}^{B}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad{w}_{p}^{A}\bar{{w}}_{qA}=\delta_{pq}\;,\qquad\det({w}_{q}^{A})=1\;.\qquad

(4.27)

and are Stückelberg fields/variables.
Until now, we consider their
q,p
indices to be transformed by
SO(7)
subgroup of
SO(8)
which preserves the unit
SO(8)
vector

k^{i}
defining the complex structure through (
4.26
). This restriction will be removed below, but presently is kept to simplify the discussion for now.

Using the bridge variables
(
2.91
) we can write a formally
SU(8)
invariant counterpart of the complex fermionic coordinates (
4.26
):

\displaystyle\Theta^{-A}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{w}_{q}^{A}(\theta^{1-}_{q}+ik\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{p}}\theta^{2-}_{\dot{q}})\;,\qquad\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bar{{w}}_{A}^{q}(\theta^{1-}_{q}-ik\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{p}}\theta^{2-}_{\dot{q}})\;.\qquad

(4.28)

Next, let us use the constant vector
k^{i}
to construct

\displaystyle{w}^{A}_{\dot{q}}={w}^{A}_{p}k\!\!\!/{}_{p\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\bar{{w}}_{\dot{q}A}=\bar{{w}}_{pA}k\!\!\!/{}_{p\dot{q}}\;.

(4.29)

This
8\times 8
matrix is also unitary and unimodular, i.e.
SU(8)
valued: one can easily check that it obeys the counterpart of (
3.52
).

Furthermore, using (
4.29
) together with (
4.27
), we can factorize the matrix (
4.25
),

\displaystyle k\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{p}}={w}_{q}^{A}\bar{{w}}_{\dot{p}A}={w}^{A}_{\dot{p}}\bar{{w}}_{qA}\;.

(4.30)

Substituting this factorization into (
4.28
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Theta^{-A}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({w}_{q}^{A}\theta^{1-}_{q}+i{w}_{\dot{q}}^{A}\theta^{2-}_{\dot{q}})\;,\qquad\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\bar{{w}}_{pA}\theta^{1-}_{q}-i\bar{{w}}_{\dot{q}A}\theta^{2-}_{\dot{q}})\;.\qquad

(4.31)

In contrast to (
4.28
), these relations can be considered to be invariant under the complete
SO(8)
acting on the
q,p
and
\dot{q},\dot{p}
indices. The
SO(8)/SO(7)
transformation would change the constant unit vector
k^{i}
(
4.25
) in
(
4.29
), but this does not matter as
k^{i}
is not included in (
4.31
).

Actually, the drawback of the above construction has been the requirement for
SO(8)
vector
k^{i}
to be constant as this breaks explicitly
SO(8)
symmetry which is essential for the definition of spinor moving frame variables.
At this stage it becomes evident that this can be avoided by requiring instead that this vector is
covariantly constant

21
21

21

Notice also that, as far as one dimensional gauge field is always purely gauge, Eq. (
4.32
) implies that the vector field
k^{i}(\tau)
can be obtained by
SO(8)
gauge
transformations starting from a constant unit vector
k^{i}_{0}
(
dk^{i}_{0}=0
).
,

Dk^{i}=dk^{i}+k^{j}\Omega^{ji}=0\;.

(4.32)

The bridge variables
w_{q}^{A}=(\bar{w}_{qA})^{*}
and
w_{\dot{q}}^{A}=(\bar{w}_{\dot{q}A})^{*}
, which define the complex structure (
4.31
), are then transformed by
the same
fundamental (and anti-fundamental) representation of
SU(8)
with s- and c-spinor representation of
SO(8)
group acting as well on the Lorentz harmonics. Thus the space of possible complex structures which can be used to quantize type IIA massless superparticle can be identified with coset
SU(8)/SO(8)
, the same one that we have found in type IIB case.

IV.2.1
Lagrangian of type IIA superparticle with manifest
SU(8)
symmetry

Eqs. (
4.29
) and (
4.30
) with real covariantly constant
k^{i}
can be used to solve Eqs. (
4.31
) with respect to the ’original’ real coordinates of the analytical basis (
4.9
),

\displaystyle\theta^{1-}_{q}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\Theta^{-A}\bar{w}_{qA}+\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}w_{q}^{A}\right)\;,\qquad\theta^{2-}_{\dot{q}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}i}\left(\Theta^{-A}\bar{w}_{\dot{q}A}-\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}w_{\dot{q}}^{A}\right)\;.\qquad

(4.33)

To obtain the Lagrangian of massless type IIA superparticle with manifest
SU(8)
symmetry, we have to substitute these expressions in the Lagrangian (
4.14
).

To present the result of such substitution in a compact and suggestive form, it is important to observe that the covariant constancy of the
SO(8)
vector
k^{i}
,
Eq. (
4.32
), implies that the derivatives of
\bar{w}_{\dot{q}A}
and
w_{\dot{q}}^{A}
, defined in Eq. (
4.29
), are expressed in terms of the same
SU(8)
Cartan forms (
2.93
) as derivatives of
\bar{w}_{{q}A}
and
w_{{q}}^{A}
, so that

\displaystyle{\cal D}{w}_{\dot{q}}{}^{A}=d{w}_{\dot{q}}{}^{A}-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}\tilde{\gamma}^{ij}_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}{\rm w}_{\dot{p}}{}^{A}-{\rm w}_{\dot{q}}{}^{A}{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}=0\;,\qquad{\cal D}\bar{{\rm w}}_{\dot{q}B}=d\bar{{\rm w}}_{\dot{q}B}-\frac{1}{4}\Omega^{ij}\gamma^{ij}_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}\bar{{\rm w}}_{\dot{p}B}+{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\bar{{\rm w}}_{\dot{q}A}=0\;.

(4.34)

With this in mind, we find that in terms of complex fermionic variables (
4.31
), the Lagrangian form of type IIA superparticle reads

\displaystyle{\cal L}_{1}^{IIA}=\rho^{\#}E^{=}=\rho^{\#}\left(DX^{=}-2i{\cal D}\Theta^{-A}\,\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}+2i\Theta^{-A}{\cal D}\bar{\Theta}^{-}_{A}\,-\Omega^{=i}X_{An}^{i}\right)\;,\qquad

(4.35)

where
{\cal D}\Theta^{-A}
and its c.c. are
SU(8)
covariant derivatives defined in (
2.101
),

\displaystyle{\cal D}\Theta^{-A}:=d\Theta^{-A}-\Theta^{-B}{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\;,\qquad{\cal D}\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{B}:=d\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{B}+{\mho}_{B}{}^{A}\bar{\Theta}{}^{-}_{A}\;.

(4.36)

Notice that the Eq. (
4.35
) coincides with (
2.100
) so that the difference between type IIA and type IIB particle is in how the complex octuplet of fermionic coordinates and its complex conjugate are related to the original Majorana–Weyl fermionic coordinate functions.

Notice that this relation in the case of type IIA superparticle inevitably involves a covariantly constant
SO(8)
vector
k^{i}
. This can be related with spacelike vector defining T-duality transformations (
4.5
) by using spacelike moving frame vectors,

k^{\mu}=k^{i}u^{\mu i}\;.

(4.37)

With such an identification, the covariant constancy of
k^{i}
, Eq. (
4.32
) follows from constancy of the space–like vector of the T-duality transformations,
dk^{a}=0
. Using the expression for derivatives of moving frame vectors in terms of Cartan forms (see Eq.
(
A.13
) in Appendix
A
) one easily finds that this latter condition also implies

k^{i}\Omega^{=i}=0\qquad{\rm and}\qquad k^{i}\Omega^{\#i}=0\;.

(4.38)

The first of these equations is satisfied on-shell as equations of motion for
X^{i}_{{\rm An}}
field are
\rho^{\#}\Omega^{=i}=0
. The second equation can be obtained by gauge fixing (complete of partial) of the
{\hbox{\mybb K}}_{8}
gauge symmetry.

Keeping this in mind, we can study the dynamical system described by the action with Lagrangian form (
4.35
) exactly in the same line as we did this with type IIB superparticle model described by Lagrangian form (
2.100
). In particular, the Hamiltonian mechanics in the complex analytical basis is identical to that described in sec.
II
and quantization results in the chiral (analytical) superfield (
III.1
).
The difference between type IIA and type IIB superparticle manifests itself when one turns to their standard spacetime description.
Namely, this happens when we associate the components of this superfields carrying antisymmetric sets of
SU(8)
indices, bosonic

\displaystyle\phi\;,\qquad\phi^{\#}_{AB}=\phi^{\#}_{[AB]}\;,\qquad\phi^{[+4]}_{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}=\frac{1}{4!\,4!}\epsilon_{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}B_{1}B_{2}B_{3}B_{4}}(\phi^{[+4]}_{B_{1}\ldots B_{4}})^{*}\;

(4.39)

and fermionic

\displaystyle\psi^{+}_{A}\;,\qquad\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC}=\psi^{[+3]}_{[ABC]}\;,\qquad

(4.40)

with the solutions of the linearized equations for the field of supergravity multiplet.

In the spinor moving frame (Lorentz harmonic) formalism the field equations of type IIA supergravity multiplet can be solved in terms of
real
bosonic and fermionic functions

\displaystyle\phi^{IIA}(p_{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})\;,\qquad c^{i}(p_{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})\;,\qquad c^{ijk}=c^{[ijk]}(x^{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})\;,\qquad h^{ij}=h^{(ij)}(x^{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})\;,\qquad h^{ii}=0\;,\qquad

(4.41)

\displaystyle\psi^{+}_{\dot{q}}(p_{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})\;,\qquad\psi^{+}_{{{q}}}(p_{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})\;,\qquad\psi^{i+}_{\dot{q}}(p_{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})\;,\qquad\psi^{i+}_{{q}}(p_{=},v_{\alpha q}^{\;-})\;,\qquad

(4.42)

by

\displaystyle C_{\mu}=-c^{i}u_{\mu}^{i}\;,\qquad C_{\mu\nu\rho}=-c^{ijk}u_{\mu}^{i}u_{\nu}^{j}u_{\rho}^{k}\;,\qquad h_{\mu\nu}=u_{\mu}^{i}u_{\nu}^{j}h^{ij}\;,\qquad

(4.43)

\displaystyle\psi^{1}_{\alpha}=\psi^{+}_{q}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\;,\qquad\psi_{\mu}^{\alpha 1}=-u_{\mu}^{i}\psi^{i+}_{\dot{q}}v_{\dot{q}}^{-\alpha}\;,\qquad\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{q}}\psi^{i-}_{\dot{q}}=0\;,\qquad

(4.44)

\displaystyle\psi^{2\alpha}=\psi^{+}_{\dot{q}}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad\psi^{2}_{\mu\alpha}=-u_{\mu}^{i}\psi^{i+}_{q}v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}\;,\qquad\psi^{i+}_{q}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{q}}=0\;.\qquad

(4.45)

The signs are introduced in such a way that the ”inverse” expressions for the on-shell fields with
SO(8)
indices in terms of spacetime fields have the natural form

\displaystyle c^{i}=C_{\mu}u^{\mu i}\;,\qquad c^{ijk}=C^{\mu\nu\rho}u_{\mu}^{i}u_{\nu}^{j}u_{\rho}^{k}\;,\qquad h^{ij}=h^{\mu\nu}u_{\mu}^{i}u_{\nu}^{j}\;,\qquad

(4.46)

\displaystyle\psi^{+}_{q}=\psi^{1}_{\alpha}v^{+\alpha}_{q}\;,\qquad\psi^{i+}_{\dot{q}}=\psi^{\mu\alpha 1}u_{\mu}^{i}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad

(4.47)

\displaystyle\psi^{+}_{\dot{q}}=\psi^{2\alpha}v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad\psi^{i+}_{q}=\psi^{2}_{\mu\alpha}u_{\mu}^{i}v^{+\alpha}_{q}\;.\qquad

(4.48)

Keeping in mind that the description of quantum spectrum of type IIA superparticle by complex chiral superfield
with field content given by (
4.39
) and (
4.40
) is reached by introducing complex structure with the use of a unit covariantly constant SO(8) vector
k^{i}
, one should not be surprised by that the correspondence with the solutions of the linearized type IIA equations in spacetime should also use this vector. Furthermore, in the light of observation that this vector can be used to define T-duality map between type IIB and type IIA superspaces, the problem of finding
the correspondence with components of state vector superfield can be equivalently formulated as a search for T-duality map between the above described solutions of linearized type IIA field equations and their type IIB counterparts discussed before.

Such a correspondence can be written with the use of unit
SO(8)
vector
k^{i}
as follows

\displaystyle\phi^{IIB}=\Re{\rm e}\phi^{IIB}+i\Im{\rm m}\phi^{IIB}

\displaystyle\qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\phi^{IIA}+ic^{i}k^{i}\;,\qquad

(4.49)

\displaystyle c^{ij}=\Re{\rm e}c^{ij}+i\Im{\rm m}c^{ij}

\displaystyle\qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle c^{ijl}k^{l}+ic^{[i}k^{j]}\;,\qquad

(4.50)

\displaystyle c^{ijkl}=-\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{ijkli^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}c^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle c^{[ijk}k^{l]}-\frac{1}{4!}\epsilon^{ijkli^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}l^{\prime}}c^{i^{\prime}j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}k^{l^{\prime}}\;,\qquad

(4.51)

\displaystyle h^{ij\,IIB}\qquad

\displaystyle\qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle{}\qquad h^{ij}\;,\qquad

(4.52)

\displaystyle\psi_{q}^{+IIB}=\Re{\rm e}\psi_{q}^{+IIB}+i\Im{\rm m}\psi_{q}^{+IIB}

\displaystyle\qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\psi_{q}^{+}+ik{}\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{q}}\psi^{+}_{\dot{q}}\;,\qquad

(4.53)

\displaystyle\psi_{\dot{q}}^{+i\;IIB}=\Re{\rm e}\psi_{\dot{q}}^{+i\;IIB}+i\Im{\rm m}\psi_{\dot{q}}^{+i\;IIB}

\displaystyle\qquad\longleftrightarrow\qquad

\displaystyle\psi_{\dot{q}}^{+i}+i\psi_{q}^{+i}k{}\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{q}}\;.\qquad

(4.54)

Then the correspondence of the components of state vector superfield
(
4.39
), (
4.40
) and on shell solutions of the linearized type IIA equations is given by (here we, for simplicity, again ignore the degrees of
\rho^{\#}
or
\partial_{=}
necessary to equalize the
SO(1,1)
weights of l.h.s. and r.h.s. of equations;
cf.
(
3.45
)– (
3.48
) and (
3.55
))

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi^{IIA}+ic^{i}k^{i}\;,\qquad

(4.55)

\displaystyle\phi_{AB}^{\#}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{16}\gamma^{ij}_{AB}c^{ijl}k^{l}+\frac{i}{16}c^{i}\gamma^{ij}_{AB}k^{j}\;,\qquad

(4.56)

\displaystyle\phi^{[+4]}_{ABCD}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\left(ic^{ijk}k^{l}t^{ijkl}_{ABCD}\;+\frac{1}{3}t^{ij}_{ABCD}h^{ij}\right)\;,

(4.57)

\displaystyle\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{8}\gamma^{ij}_{[AB}\gamma^{j}_{C]D}(\psi_{\dot{q}}^{+i}w_{\dot{q}}{}^{D}+i\psi_{q}^{+}w_{{q}}{}^{D})\;,\qquad

(4.58)

\displaystyle\psi^{[+]}_{A}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{q}^{+}\bar{w}_{{q}A}+i\psi_{\dot{q}}^{+}\bar{w}_{\dot{q}A}\;,\qquad

(4.59)

When writing the above equations we have used (
4.29
) and (anti-)self-duality properties of
t
-symbols (
3.49
).

These relations can be resolved by expressions for solutions of linearized type IIA equations in terms of components of the state vector superfield,

\displaystyle h^{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{16}\bar{t}^{ij\;ABCD}\phi^{[+4]}_{ABCD}\;,\qquad

(4.60)

\displaystyle c^{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Im{\rm m}\left(2\bar{\gamma}{}^{ij\,AB}k^{j}\phi^{[+2]}_{AB}+\phi k^{i}\right)\;,\qquad

(4.61)

\displaystyle c^{ijk}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{i}{4}k^{l}\bar{t}^{lijk\;ABCD}\phi^{[+4]}_{ABCD}+3k^{[i}\,\Re{\rm e}(\bar{\gamma}{}^{jk]\,AB}\phi^{[+2]}_{AB})\;,\qquad

(4.62)

\displaystyle\psi^{+i}_{\dot{q}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{6}\Re{\rm e}(\bar{\gamma}{}^{ij\,AB}\bar{\gamma}{}^{j\,CD}\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC}\bar{w}_{\dot{q}D})\;,\qquad\psi^{+i}_{{q}}=\frac{1}{6}\Im{\rm m}(\bar{\gamma}{}^{ij\,AB}\bar{\gamma}{}^{j\,CD}\psi^{[+3]}_{ABC}\bar{w}_{{q}D})\;,\qquad

(4.63)

\displaystyle\psi^{+}_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Re{\rm e}(\psi^{[+]}_{A}w_{{q}}{}^{A})\;,\qquad\psi^{+}_{\dot{q}}=\Re{\rm e}(\psi^{[+]}_{A}w_{\dot{q}}{}^{A})\;,\qquad

(4.64)

Thus, we have established the one-to-one correspondence between
the field content of
the
state vector superfield and
the
solutions of the linearized equations of type IIA supergravity. This correspondence inevitably involves a unit covariantly constant SO(8) vector
k^{i}
which was also used to introduce complex structure in the phase space of type IIA superparticle and, through (
4.37
), to define T-duality transformations between type IIA and type IIB supergravities. The presence of this unit
8
–vector makes the relation between spacetime fields and components of state vector superfield a bit
convoluted. In particular the degrees of freedom of vector and third rank antisymmetric tensor are redistributed between different spin-tensor fields.

Notice that in the on-shell superfield description of quantum state spectrum of type IIA superparticle, when quantized in the frame of its spinor moving frame formulation by the method we followed in this paper, possesses
SU(8)
symmetry which is hidden in its standard spacetime (and standard superspace) description.

V
On amplitudes and superamplitudes in type II theories

In this section we will firstly discuss and reproduce in our notation some simple amplitudes and superamplitudes of type IIB supergravity studied in
Boels:2012ie
;
Boels:2012zr
and discuss their type IIA counterparts. Then we discuss some simple amplitudes of type IIA theory involving D
0
–branes and point out the issue which hampers the way to superamplitudes in this case.

V.1
Spinor moving frame approach to spinor helicity formalism

The spinor moving frame approach to the spinor helicity formalism in
D=10
(and in
D=11
) was developed in
Bandos:2017zap
;
Bandos:2017eof
. The spinor helicity variables and polarization spinors of
{\rm i}
-th of
n
massless scattered particles (
{\rm i}=1,...,n
),
\lambda_{\alpha q{\rm i}}
and
\lambda^{\alpha}_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}
are related to the spinor moving frame
(v^{-}_{\alpha{q}{\rm i}},v^{+}_{\alpha\dot{q}{\rm i}})
attached to the light–like momentum of this particle in the sense of

p_{\mu{\rm i}}=\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}u_{\mu{\rm i}}^{=}\;,

(5.1)

and to its inverse defined in (
A.1
), by

\displaystyle\lambda_{\alpha{q}{\rm i}}=\sqrt{|\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}|}v^{-}_{\alpha{q}{\rm i}}\;,\qquad\lambda^{\alpha}_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}=\sqrt{|\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}|}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}\;.\qquad

(5.2)

The complementary set of spinors used in
Boels:2012ie
,
\xi_{\alpha\dot{q}{\rm i}}
and
\xi^{\alpha}_{{q}{\rm i}}
in our index notation, can be associated with second rectangular block of spinor moving frame matrix (
2.10
) and its inverse (
A.1
),

\displaystyle\xi_{\alpha{q}{\rm i}}\;\propto\;v^{+}_{\alpha\dot{q}{\rm i}}\;,\qquad\xi^{\alpha}_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}\propto v^{+\alpha}_{q{\rm i}}\;.

(5.3)

Each spinor moving frame is defined up to its associated
\{[SO(1,1)\otimes SO(8)]\subset\!\!\!\!\!\!\times{\hbox{\mybb K}}_{8}\}
gauge symmetry, which we denote by
\{[SO(1,1)\otimes SO(8)]\subset\!\!\!\!\!\!\times{\hbox{\mybb K}}_{8}\}_{{\rm i}}
for
{\rm i}
-th particle. These can be used (see
Bandos:2017eof
) to gauge fix its decomposition

\displaystyle v_{\alpha q\,{\rm i}}^{\;-}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v_{\alpha q}^{\;-}+{1\over 2}K^{=i}_{{\rm i}}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}v_{\alpha\dot{p}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad v_{\alpha\dot{q}\,{\rm i}}^{\;+}=v_{\alpha\dot{q}}^{\;+}\;,\qquad

(5.4)

\displaystyle v^{+\alpha}_{q\,{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle v^{+\alpha}_{q}\;,\qquad v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}\,{\rm i}}=v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}-{1\over 2}K^{=i}_{{\rm i}}v^{+\alpha}_{q}\gamma^{i}_{{q}\dot{q}}\;\qquad

(5.5)

with respect to some reference spinor moving frame
(v^{-}_{\alpha{q}},v^{+}_{\alpha\dot{q}})
which, in its turn, is defined up to associated
[SO(1,1)\otimes SO(8)]\subset\!\!\!\!\!\!\times{\hbox{\mybb K}}_{8}
gauge transformations. Only this symmetry preserves the gauge (
5.4
).

Notice that, in particular, the spinor moving frame of one of the scattered particles can be chosen as reference frame.

The corresponding gauge fixed version of
{\rm i}
-th vector frame reads

\displaystyle u^{=}_{\mu{\rm i}}=u^{=}_{\mu}+K^{=j}_{{\rm i}}u^{j}_{\mu}+\frac{1}{4}(\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm i}})^{2}u^{\#}_{\mu}\;,\qquad

(5.6)

\displaystyle u^{j}_{\mu{\rm i}}=u^{j}_{\mu}+\frac{1}{2}K^{=j}_{{\rm i}}\,u^{\#}_{\mu}\;,\qquad

(5.7)

\displaystyle u^{\#}_{\mu{\rm i}}=u^{\#}_{\mu}\;.\qquad

(5.8)

In the gauge (
5.4
) the characteristic contractions of the
helicity spinors and polarization spinors is expressed by

\displaystyle<{\rm i}\;^{-}_{q}\;|\;{\rm j}\;^{-}_{\dot{p}}\;>\;:=\;v_{\alpha q\,{\rm i}}^{\;-}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{p}\,{\rm j}}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\;K^{=k}_{{\rm i}{\rm j}}\gamma^{k}_{q\dot{p}}\;,

(5.9)

where

\vec{K}{}^{=}_{{\rm ij}}:=\vec{K}{}^{=}_{{\rm i}}-\vec{K}{}^{=}_{{\rm j}}=\frac{1}{4}\,<{\rm i}\;^{-}_{q}\;|\;{\rm j}\;^{-}_{\dot{p}}\;>\;\vec{\gamma}_{q\dot{p}}\;\;.

(5.10)

Notice also that
u_{\mu{\rm i}}^{=}u^{\mu=}_{{\rm j}}=\frac{1}{2}\,(\vec{K}_{{\rm ij}}^{=})^{2}
.

The above construction was elaborated and generalized to
11D
in
Bandos:2017eof
. However, for our present purposes it is more convenient to introduce complex helicity spinors and polarization spinors, along the lines
initiated in
Bandos:2017zap
and continued in
Bandos:2019zqp
. In the light of our above observation on hidden
SU(8)
symmetry of the linearized supergravity which appears naturally in superparticle quantization, here we further elaborate it in its
SU(8)
covariant form.

The complex helicity spinors and polarization spinors are defined by

\displaystyle{\lambda}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}=\sqrt{|\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}|}\bar{v}{}^{-}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}:=\sqrt{|\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}|}v^{-}_{\alpha{q}{\rm i}}\bar{w}_{qA{\rm i}}\;,\qquad\lambda^{\alpha A}_{{\rm i}}=\sqrt{|\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}|}v^{-\alpha A}_{{\rm i}}=\sqrt{|\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}|}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}w_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}^{\;A}\;.

(5.11)

These variables are sufficient to form the complete basis of spinors of opposite chiralities with
{v}{}^{+A}_{\alpha{\rm i}}:=v^{+}_{\alpha\dot{q}{\rm i}}w_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}^{\;A}
and
\bar{v}{}^{+\alpha}_{A{\rm i}}:=v{}^{+\alpha}_{{q}{\rm i}}\bar{w}_{qA{\rm i}}
. However, the expression for momenta in terms of spinors (
5.11
) includes also their complex conjugates, so that we shall use the element of all the overfull basis

\displaystyle\bar{v}{}^{-}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}=v^{-}_{\alpha{q}{\rm i}}\bar{w}_{qA{\rm i}}\;,\qquad\bar{v}{}^{+}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}=v^{+}_{\alpha\dot{q}{\rm i}}\bar{w}_{\dot{q}A{\rm i}}\;,\qquad{}\qquad{v}{}^{-A}_{\alpha{\rm i}}=v^{-}_{\alpha{q}{\rm i}}{w}^{\,A}_{q{\rm i}}\;,\qquad{v}{}^{+A}_{\alpha{\rm i}}=v^{+}_{\alpha\dot{q}{\rm i}}{w}^{\;A}_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}\;,\qquad

(5.12)

\displaystyle v^{-\alpha A}_{{\rm i}}=v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}w_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}^{\;A}\;,\qquad v^{+\alpha A}_{{\rm i}}=v^{+\alpha}_{{q}{\rm i}}w_{{q}{\rm i}}^{\;A}\;,\qquad{}\qquad\bar{v}{}^{-\alpha}_{A{\rm i}}=v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}\bar{w}_{qA{\rm i}}\;,\qquad\bar{v}{}^{+\alpha}_{A{\rm i}}=v^{+\alpha}_{{q}{\rm i}}\bar{w}_{{q}A{\rm i}}^{\;A}\;.\qquad

(5.13)

The gauge fixing of symmetries the set of which in this case includes also
SU(8)_{{\rm i}}
groups, allows us to identify all the bridge variables for each of scattered particles with the reference bridge variables,

\displaystyle\bar{w}_{qA{\rm i}}=\bar{w}_{qA}\;,\qquad w_{{q}{\rm i}}^{\;A}=w_{{q}}^{\;A}\;,\qquad{}\qquad\bar{w}_{\dot{q}A{\rm i}}=\bar{w}_{\dot{q}A}\;,\qquad w_{\dot{q}{\rm i}}^{\;A}=w_{\dot{q}}^{\;A}\;,\qquad

(5.14)

and to arrive at the following expressions for complex spinor frame variables in terms of reference complex spinor frame

\displaystyle\bar{v}{}^{-}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{v}{}^{-}_{\alpha A}+{1\over 2}K^{=j}_{{\rm i}}\gamma^{j}_{AB}v_{\alpha}^{+B}\;,\qquad v_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{+B}=v_{\alpha}^{+B}\;,\qquad

(5.15)

\displaystyle\bar{v}{}^{+\alpha}_{A{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bar{v}{}^{+\alpha}_{A}\;,\qquad v^{-A\alpha}_{{\rm i}}=v^{-A\alpha}-{1\over 2}K^{=j}_{{\rm i}}\tilde{\bar{\gamma}}{}^{j\,AB}\bar{v}^{+\alpha}_{B}\;.\qquad

(5.16)

Here
\gamma^{i}_{AB}
and
\bar{\gamma}{}^{iAB}
carrying
SO(8)
vector index and two indices of fundamental anti-fundamental representations of
SU(8)
are defined in (
3.53
). Eqs. (
5.15
) and (
5.16
) are covariant with respect to only one copy of
SU(8)\otimes SO(8)\otimes SO(1,1)
symmetry acting on the reference spinor moving frame and related reference bridge variables only.

The convenience of such a complex choice of helicity spinors and associated spinor moving frame is that it can be used to write a simple representation of complex generators of type IIB supersymmetry acting on the on-shell superfields and also on superamplitudes:

\displaystyle\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(Q_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{1}+iQ_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{2})=v_{\alpha q\,{\rm i}}^{\;-}w_{q{\rm i}}^{A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta_{{\rm i}}^{-A}}=v_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{-A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta_{{\rm i}}^{-A}}\;,\qquad

(5.17)

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(Q_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{1}-iQ_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{2})=4\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}v_{\alpha q\,{\rm i}}^{\;-}\bar{w}_{qA{\rm i}}\Theta_{{\rm i}}^{-A}=4\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}\bar{v}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}^{\;-}\Theta_{{\rm i}}^{-A}\;.\qquad

(5.18)

The relation of
{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}
and
\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}
through Hermitian conjugation is not straightforward in this representation (like it is in its 4D counterpart, see e.g.
Brandhuber:2008pf
;
Arkani-Hamed:2008gz
as well as
Boels:2012ie
and refs therein). However, the superalgebra of type IIB supersymmetry (
2.5
) is reproduced

\displaystyle\{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}\;,\;{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta{\rm j}}\}=0\;,\qquad\{\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}\;,\;\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta{\rm j}}\}=0\;,\qquad\{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}\;,\;\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta{\rm j}}\}=2\delta_{{\rm i}{\rm j}}\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\beta}p_{a{\rm i}}\;,\qquad p_{a{\rm i}}=\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}u^{=}_{a{\rm i}}\;.\qquad

(5.19)

Notice that (
5.19
) reflects also the fact that supercharges of different particles anticommute. This is important, for instance, when calculating the algebra of complete supercharges of the system of
n
scattered massless (super)particles (below referred to as ”supergravitons”)

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha}=\sum\limits_{{\rm i}=1}^{n}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}\;,\qquad\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta}=\sum\limits_{{\rm j}=1}^{n}\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta{\rm j}}\;\qquad

(5.20)

which is

\displaystyle\{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha}\;,\;{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta}\}=0\;,\qquad\{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha}\;,\;\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta}\}=2\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}{\hbox{\mybb P}}_{\mu}\;,\qquad\{\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\alpha}\;,\;\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta}\}=0\;,\qquad

(5.21)

with the total momentum given by

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb P}}_{\mu}:=\sum\limits_{{\rm i}=1}^{n}p_{\mu{\rm i}}=\sum\limits_{{\rm i}=1}^{n}\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}u^{=}_{\mu{\rm i}}\;.\qquad

(5.22)

Notice that in discussion of
10D
SYM and
11D
SUGRA amplitudes in
Caron-Huot:2010nes
;
Bandos:2017zap
;
Bandos:2017eof

the counterpart of
{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha}=4\sum\limits_{{\rm i}=1}\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}v^{-}_{\alpha q{\rm i}}\bar{w}_{qA}\Theta^{-A}_{{\rm i}}
was called supermomentum.
Under supersymmetry it transforms as

\displaystyle\delta_{susy}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha}=\sqrt{2}\,\left(\sum\limits_{{\rm i}=1}^{n}p_{\mu{\rm i}}\right)\,\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}(\epsilon^{1\beta}+i\epsilon^{2\beta})

(5.23)

and, hence, is invariant under supersymmetry if the momentum is conserved,
\sum\limits_{{\rm i}=1}^{n}p_{\mu{\rm i}}=0
. This statement is tantamount to that for conserved momentum (
5.21
) represents complex Grassmann algebra.

The counterpart of (
5.9
) in complex spinor frame variables reads

\displaystyle<{\rm i}\;^{-}_{A}\;|\;{\rm j}\;^{-}_{B}\;\;>\;:=\;\bar{v}{}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}^{\;-}\bar{v}{}^{-\alpha}_{B\;{\rm j}}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\;K^{=k}_{{\rm i}{\rm j}}\gamma^{k}_{AB}\;,\qquad<{\rm i}\;^{-}_{A}\;|\;{\rm j}\;^{-B}\;\;>\;:=\;\bar{v}{}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}^{\;-}{v}{}^{-B\alpha}_{\;{\rm j}}\;=0\;,\qquad

(5.24)

\displaystyle<{\rm i}\;^{-A}\;|\;{\rm j}\;^{-}_{B}\;\;>\;:=\;v_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{-A}\bar{v}{}^{-\alpha}_{B\;{\rm j}}\;=0\;,\qquad<{\rm i}\;^{-A}\;|\;{\rm j}\;^{-B}\;\;>\;:=\;{v}{}_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{-A}{v}{}^{-B\alpha}_{\;{\rm j}}\;=\;\frac{1}{2}\;K^{=k}_{{\rm i}{\rm j}}\tilde{\gamma}^{k\,BA}\;.\qquad

(5.25)

V.2
Supersymmetric Ward identities and their solutions

As it was discussed in
Boels:2012ie
,
Wang:2015jna
and
Kallosh:2024lsl
, following the
4D
approach of
Arkani-Hamed:2008gz
;
Elvang:2009wd
, the Ward identities for n–particle superamplitude of type IIB supergravity,
{\cal A}_{n}
,

{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha}\;{\cal A}_{n}=0\;,\qquad\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\beta}\;{\cal A}_{n}=0\;,\qquad

(5.26)

for the case of
n\geq 4
are solved by

{\cal A}_{n}=\delta^{16}({\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha})\,\delta^{10}({\hbox{\mybb P}}_{\mu})\,\tilde{{\cal B}}_{n}\equiv\delta^{16}({Q}_{\alpha})\,\delta^{10}({\hbox{\mybb P}}_{\mu})\,{\cal B}_{n},

(5.27)

where
{\cal B}_{n}={\cal B}_{n}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}},v^{-}_{{\rm i}},\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}})
obeys

\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\alpha}\,{\cal B}_{n}=0\;.

(5.28)

In (
5.27
)
\delta^{16}({\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha})
is Grassmann delta function,

\delta^{16}({\hbox{\mybb Q}})=({\hbox{\mybb Q}})^{16}={\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{1}\ldots{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{16}=\frac{1}{16!}\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{16}}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{1}}\ldots{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{16}}\;.

(5.29)

Notice that the Ward identities (
5.26
) imply momentum preservation in the scattering process under consideration,

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb P}}_{\mu}:=\sum\limits_{{\rm i}=1}^{n}p_{\mu{\rm i}}=\sum\limits_{{\rm i}=1}^{n}\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}u^{=}_{\mu{\rm i}}=0\;,\qquad

(5.30)

and this is reflected by including
\delta^{10}({\hbox{\mybb P}}_{\mu})
into their
solution (
5.27
).

In
Wang:2015jna
and
Kallosh:2024lsl

three solutions of (
5.28
), or better to say of (
5.26
), were considered:

•

The first, called 1/2 BPS in
Kallosh:2024lsl
, contains
\Theta^{-A}
– independent
{\cal B}_{n}={\cal B}_{n}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}},v^{-}_{{\rm i}})
.

•

The second is with supersymmetric conjugate amplitude

\overline{{\cal A}}_{n}=\,\delta^{10}({\hbox{\mybb P}}_{\mu})\,\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}^{16}\overline{\tilde{{\cal B}}}_{n}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}},v^{-}_{{\rm i}},\partial/\partial\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}})\prod\limits_{{\rm i}=1}^{n}(\rho^{\#}_{i})^{4}(\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}})^{\wedge 8}\;,

(5.31)

where

(\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}})^{\wedge 8}=\frac{1}{8}\epsilon_{A_{1}\ldots A_{8}}\Theta^{-A_{1}}_{{\rm i}}\ldots\Theta^{-A_{8}}_{{\rm i}}\;.

(5.32)

•

The third is with
{\cal B}_{n}
given by the image of maximal power of

{\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}}_{\alpha}
operator,

{\cal B}_{n}=(\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}})^{16}\,{\cal C}{}_{n}\;.

(5.33)

The case of
n=3
is special in several respects. In particular, the dimensional analysis excludes appearance of a complete Grassmann delta functions in the three point superamplitude. The study of this case in the frame of spinor moving frame approach, besides being illuminating, provides
us with a basis for further discussion of type IIA amplitudes.

V.3
Three particle kinematics and three particle on-shell amplitudes of type IIB SUGRA

Let us apply spinor moving frame formalism to the analysis of three particle kinematics.
Following
Bandos:2017eof
, we find that the momentum conservation in scattering of three massless particles, Eq. (
5.30
) with
n=3
,
gives three equations two of which are solved by

\displaystyle\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}}=-(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 2}}+\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 3}})\;,

(5.34)

\displaystyle K^{=i}_{{\rm 21}}:=K^{=i}_{{\rm 2}}-K^{=i}_{{\rm 1}}=-\frac{\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 3}}}{\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 2}}}K^{=i}_{{\rm 31}}\;,

(5.35)

and the third one
requires

\displaystyle(\vec{K}{}^{=}_{{\rm 31}})^{2}:=K^{=i}_{{\rm 31}}K^{=i}_{{\rm 31}}=0\;.\qquad

(5.36)

Clearly, (
5.36
) has a nontrivial solution if
the
SO(8)
vector
K^{=i}_{{\rm 31}}
is complex. Then, if we exclude collinear scattering, this complex null-vector is non vanishing,

K^{=i}_{{\rm 31}}\not=0
, and hence
|\vec{K}{}^{=}_{{\rm 31}}|^{2}:=K^{=i}_{{\rm 31}}(K^{=i}_{{\rm 31}})^{*}\not=0
.
With this restriction, the complex matrix
\hat{K}_{q\dot{p}}:=\frac{1}{|\vec{K}{}^{=}_{{\rm 31}}|}\,K^{=j}_{{\rm 31}}\gamma^{j}_{q\dot{p}}
is nilpotent
\hat{K}(\hat{K})^{T}=0
, but in
its product with its Hermitian conjugate factorizes a real projector,

\displaystyle(\hat{K}\hat{K}^{\dagger})_{qp}=2{\cal P}_{qp}\,,\qquad((\hat{K})^{\dagger}\hat{K})_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}=\tilde{{\cal P}}_{\dot{q}\dot{p}}\;,\qquad

(5.37)

\displaystyle{\cal P}{\cal P}={\cal P}\;,\qquad\tilde{{\cal P}}\tilde{{\cal P}}=\tilde{{\cal P}}\;.

(5.38)

This implies that matrix
\hat{K}_{q\dot{p}}
has rank 4
i.e.

it can be factorized as

\hat{K}_{q\dot{p}}:=\frac{1}{|\vec{K}{}^{=}_{{\rm 31}}|}\,K^{=j}_{{\rm 31}}\gamma^{j}_{q\dot{p}}=2\beta_{{\rm 31}}{\rm s}_{q}{}^{\tilde{A}}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{p}\tilde{A}}\;\qquad\beta_{{\rm 31}}=\pm={\rm{sign}}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 3}}\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})\;,\qquad\tilde{A}=1,...,4\;,\qquad

(5.39)

where the role of sign multiplier

\beta_{{\rm 31}}:={\rm{sign}}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 3}}\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})
will become clear latter and

{\rm s}_{q}{}^{\tilde{A}}
and
\bar{{\rm s}}_{\dot{p}\tilde{A}}
are complex
8\times 4
matrices which obey

\displaystyle{\rm s}_{q}{}^{\tilde{A}}{\rm s}_{q}{}^{\tilde{B}}=0\;,\qquad{\rm s}_{q}{}^{\tilde{A}}\bar{{\rm s}}_{q\tilde{B}}=\delta_{\tilde{B}}{}^{\tilde{A}}\;,\qquad\bar{{\rm s}}_{q\tilde{A}}\bar{{\rm s}}_{q\tilde{B}}=0\;,\qquad

(5.40)

\displaystyle\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{p}}{}^{\tilde{A}}\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{p}}{}^{\tilde{B}}=0\;,\qquad\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{p}}{}^{\tilde{B}}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{p}\tilde{A}}=\delta_{\tilde{A}}{}^{\tilde{B}}\;,\qquad\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{p}\tilde{A}}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{p}\tilde{B}}=0\;,\qquad

(5.41)

\displaystyle\tilde{A},\tilde{B}=1,2,3,4\;,\qquad q,p=1,\ldots,8\;,\qquad\dot{q},\dot{p}=1,\ldots,8\;.

At this stage it might seem that tilde on
\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{q}}{}^{\tilde{A}}=(\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{q}\tilde{A}})^{*}
is redundant as its difference with
{\rm s}
is reflected by c-spinor index of
SO(8)

vs
s-spinor index of
{\rm s}_{{q}}{}^{\tilde{A}}=(\bar{{\rm s}}_{q\tilde{A}})^{*}
. However, this difference becomes useful when we convert
SO(8)

indices into
SU(8)
ones,
the same as carried by components of the state vector superfield(s),
thus arriving at

\displaystyle{\rm s}_{B}{}^{\tilde{A}}=\bar{w}_{qB}{\rm s}_{{q}}{}^{\tilde{A}}=(\bar{{\rm s}}^{B}{}_{\tilde{A}})^{*}=(w_{q}^{B}\bar{{\rm s}}_{q\tilde{A}})^{*}\;,\qquad{\rm s}{}^{B\tilde{A}}=\bar{w}_{qB}{\rm s}_{{q}}{}^{\tilde{A}}=(\bar{{\rm s}}_{B\tilde{A}})^{*}=(\bar{w}_{qB}\bar{{\rm s}}_{q\tilde{A}})^{*}\;,\qquad

(5.42)

\displaystyle\tilde{{\rm s}}_{B}{}^{\tilde{A}}=\bar{w}_{\dot{q}B}\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{q}}{}^{\tilde{A}}=(\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}{}^{B}_{\tilde{A}})^{*}=(w_{\dot{q}}{}^{B}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{q}\tilde{A}})^{*}\;,\qquad\tilde{{\rm s}}{}^{B\tilde{A}}=w_{\dot{q}}{}^{B}\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{q}}{}^{\tilde{A}}=(\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{B\tilde{A}})^{*}=(\bar{w}_{\dot{q}B}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{q}\tilde{A}})^{*}\;.

(5.43)

Eqs. (
5.40
) mean that both of these complex
8\times 4
matrices,
{\rm s}_{{q}}{}^{\tilde{A}}=(\bar{{\rm s}}_{q\tilde{A}})^{*}
and
\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{q}}{}^{\tilde{A}}=(\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{q}\tilde{A}})^{*}
, actually parametrize SO(8) group. Furthermore, taking into account that factorization is invariant under
SU(4)=Spin(6)
gauge transformations, they can be treated as a kind of homogeneous coordinates for
SO(8)/SU(4)\approx SO(8)/SO(6)
coset. This is a reflection of the fact that the presence of complex null
8
–vector in our three-body (better three-wave) problem breaks the
SO(8)
symmetry down to
SO(6)
.

Notice that at this point our moving frame based approach becomes different from that of
Boels:2012ie

in which a set of 3 different matrices with indices of small group
SO(8)

and tiny group
SU(4)\approx SO(6)
is introduced. This results in an ambiguity in an invariant tensor, which then has to be fixed.

The basic contractions of helicity and polarization spinors with
SO(8)
indices are now expressed by universal formula

\displaystyle<{\rm i}^{-}_{q}\;|\;{\rm j}^{-}_{\dot{q}}>=-<{\rm j}^{-}_{q}\;|\;{\rm i}^{-}_{\dot{q}}>=\,\beta_{{\rm ij}}\,|\vec{K}{}_{{\rm i}{\rm j}}^{=}|\,{\rm s}_{q}{}^{\tilde{A}}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{p}\tilde{A}}\;,\qquad{\rm i}\,<\,{\rm j}=1,2,3\;,

(5.44)

where the last inequality is understood
{\rm mod}(3)
, i.e. in the sense of cyclic order, with
3<1
.

Passing to variables with
SU(8)
indices, we find
from (
5.44
)

\displaystyle<{\rm i}^{-}_{B}\;|\;{\rm j}^{-}_{A}>=-<{\rm j}^{-}_{B}\;|\;{\rm i}^{-}_{A}>=\,\beta_{{\rm ij}}\,|\vec{K}{}_{{\rm i}{\rm j}}^{=}|\,{\rm s}_{B}{}^{\tilde{C}}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{A\tilde{C}}\;,\qquad{\rm i}\,<\,{\rm j}=1,2,3\;,

(5.45)

In our approach the complex invariant tensor 4-th rank antisymmetric spin–tensor with SO(8) c- and s-spinor indices are defined uniquely by

\displaystyle\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{q}_{1}\ldots\dot{q}_{4}}=\,\frac{1}{4!}\,\epsilon_{\tilde{A}_{1}\ldots\tilde{A}_{4}}\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{q}_{1}}{}^{\tilde{A}_{1}}\ldots\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{q}_{4}}{}^{\tilde{A}_{4}}=(\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{q}_{1}\ldots\dot{q}_{4}})^{*}=:\tilde{{\rm s}}{}^{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}\bar{w}_{\dot{q}_{1}A_{1}}\ldots\bar{w}_{\dot{q}_{4}A_{4}}\;,\qquad

(5.46)

\displaystyle{{\rm s}}_{{q}_{1}\ldots{q}_{4}}=\,\frac{1}{4!}\,\epsilon_{\tilde{A}_{1}\ldots\tilde{A}_{4}}{{\rm s}}_{{q}_{1}}{}^{\tilde{A}_{1}}\ldots{{\rm s}}_{{q}_{4}}{}^{\tilde{A}_{4}}=(\bar{{{\rm s}}}_{{q}_{1}\ldots{q}_{4}})^{*}=:{{\rm s}}{}^{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}\bar{w}_{{q}_{1}A_{1}}\ldots\bar{w}_{{q}_{4}A_{4}}\;\;.\qquad

(5.47)

Notice that

\epsilon_{{q}_{1}\ldots{q}_{4}{p}_{1}\ldots{p}_{4}}{{{\rm s}}}{}_{{q}_{1}\ldots{q}_{4}}\bar{{{\rm s}}}{}_{{p}_{1}\ldots{p}_{4}}=1\;,\qquad\hat{K}_{q_{1}[\dot{q}_{1}|}\ldots\hat{K}_{q_{4}|\dot{q}_{4}]}=2\cdot 4!\,{\rm s}_{q_{1}\ldots q_{4}}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{q}_{1}\ldots\dot{q}_{4}}\;.\qquad

(5.48)

Below, to relate amplitudes and superamplitudes, we will use the relation

\displaystyle-\hat{K}^{i}K^{j}t^{ij}_{qprs}=\hat{K}_{[q|\dot{q}}\hat{K}_{|p|\dot{p}}{\gamma}{}^{i}_{|r|\dot{q}}{\gamma}{}^{i}_{|s]\dot{p}}=8\cdot 4!\,{\rm s}_{qprs}\;

(5.49)

which was presented (in a slightly different notation) and was used essentially in
Boels:2012ie
.
We refer to Appendix
D
for more discussion and for technical details.

The covariant spin–tensor (
5.46
) can be used to construct the invariant expression for 12-th power of
supercharge (
5.18
),

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{{12}}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{12!}\,\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{16}}{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{q}_{1}\ldots\dot{q}_{4}}\,(\rho^{\#})^{-2}v^{+}_{\alpha_{1}\dot{q}_{1}}\ldots v^{+}_{\alpha_{4}\dot{q}_{4}}\,{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{5}}\ldots{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{16}}\;\qquad

(5.50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{12!}\,\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{16}}{\tilde{{\rm s}}}{}^{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}\,(\rho^{\#})^{-2}v^{+}_{\alpha_{1}A_{1}}\ldots v^{+}_{\alpha_{4}A_{4}}\,{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{5}}\ldots{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{16}}\;\;.\qquad

Here we introduced a compensator
\rho^{\#}
for the
SO(1,1)
symmetry of the reference spinor moving frame used in
(
5.4
) –
(
5.8
).
This compensator can be identified with any of
\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}
, e.g. with
\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}}
.

The invariant (
5.50
) can be used to write the expression for 3-particle
(3-wave) superamplitude

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{3}={\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{{12}}{\cal B}_{3}\;,\qquad\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\alpha}{\cal B}_{3}=0\;.

(5.51)

To specify it we, following
Boels:2012ie
, are going to use knowledge of one of particular basic 3-point amplitudes describing the process involving two scalar fields and a graviton.
This amplitude in its turn can be constructed from the amplitude involving two scalars and one vector field using KLT (Kawai–Lewellen–Tye) relation
Kawai:1985xq
.

The two-scalar one vector amplitude can be written in the form (see e.g.
Boels:2012ie
for
D=8
case)

A(\bar{\phi}c^{i}\phi)=p_{{\rm 1}\mu}u^{\mu i}_{{\rm 2}}

(5.52)

where
p_{{\rm 1}\mu}
is the momentum of the first (scalar) particle and
u^{\mu i}_{{\rm 2}}
is the polarization vector of
the
second, vector particle which can be identified with the ”orthogonal” to momentum vectors of the associated moving frame.
This amplitude is relevant for the case of
10D
SYM theory, and can be discussed in the context of type IIA theory, but in type IIB case its calculation is an intermediate stage.

In the spinor moving frame approach we can further
specify this amplitude as

\displaystyle A(\bar{\phi}c^{i}\phi)=p_{{\rm 1}\mu}u^{\mu i}_{{\rm 2}}=\frac{1}{4}\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}}<\,{\rm 2}^{-}_{q}\,|\,{\rm 1}^{-}_{\dot{p}}\,>\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}=-\frac{1}{4}\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}}\,|\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm 21}}|\,\beta_{{\rm 21}}{\rm s}_{q}{}^{\tilde{A}}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{p}\tilde{A}}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}\;\qquad

(5.53)

which implies

\displaystyle A(\bar{\phi}c^{i}\phi)\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}}<\,{\rm 1}^{-}_{q}\,|\,{\rm 2}^{-}_{\dot{p}}\,>=-2\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}}\,|\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm 21}}|\,\beta_{{\rm 21}}{\rm s}_{q}{}^{\tilde{A}}\bar{\tilde{{\rm s}}}_{\dot{p}\tilde{A}}\;.\qquad

(5.54)

Now, following
Kawai:1985xq
, we have to write the amplitude for the process with two scalars and one graviton as a kind of product of two (
5.52
),

\displaystyle A(\bar{\phi}h^{ij}\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p_{{\rm 1}\mu}u^{\mu i}_{{\rm 2}}p_{{\rm 3}\nu}u^{\nu i}_{{\rm 2}}=\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}}\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 3}}u^{=}_{{\rm 1}\mu}u^{\mu i}_{{\rm 2}}u^{=}_{{\rm 3}\nu}u^{\nu j}_{{\rm 2}}=-(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{2}K^{=i}_{{\rm 21}}K^{=j}_{{\rm 21}}\;.

(5.55)

One can immediately check that the
r.h.s.
of this expression is traceless on its
SO(8)
v-indices, as the
l.h.s.
should be.

In our approach, the natural combination to be constructed from (
5.52
) is

\displaystyle A(\bar{\phi}h_{ABCD}\phi)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle A(\bar{\phi}h^{ij}\phi)t^{ij}_{ABCD}=p_{{\rm 1}\mu}u^{\mu i}_{{\rm 2}}p_{{\rm 3}\nu}u^{\nu j}_{{\rm 2}}t^{ij}_{ABCD}=-(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{2}K^{=i}_{{\rm 21}}K^{=j}_{{\rm 21}}t^{ij}_{ABCD}\;.

(5.56)

Notice that this is a ”self-dual” or real part of the amplitude involving
\phi_{ABCD}
component of the state vector superfield (which is a complex field obeying (
3.21
)).
The ’anti-self dual’ or imaginary part of the amplitude
A(\bar{\phi}\phi_{ABCD}\phi)
, should be expressed through the amplitude of the self-dual
field antisymmetric 4-rank
SO(8)
tensor,

A(\bar{\phi}c^{ijkl}\phi)
. However this vanishes just because it is impossible to reproduce its tensor structure using two light-like momenta,
p_{{\rm 1}\mu}
and
p_{{\rm 3}\mu}
and polarization vector
u^{i\mu}_{{\rm 2}}
only.

Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle A(\bar{\phi}^{[+8]}\phi^{[+4]}_{ABCD}\phi)=\,(\rho^{\#}_{\rm 1})^{4}\,(\rho^{\#}_{\rm 2})^{2}\,p_{{\rm 1}\mu}u^{\mu i}_{{\rm 2}}p_{{\rm 3}\nu}u^{\nu j}_{{\rm 2}}t^{ij}_{ABCD}=-(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{6}\,(\rho^{\#}_{\rm 2})^{2}\,K^{=i}_{{\rm 21}}K^{=j}_{{\rm 21}}t^{ij}_{ABCD}=\qquad

\displaystyle=\,8\,4!\,(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{6}\,(\rho^{\#}_{\rm 2})^{2}\,|\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm 12}}|^{2}\,{{\rm s}}_{ABCD}\;,\qquad

(5.57)

where at the last stage Eq. (
5.49
) was used. Notice that in this equation we have indicated the SO(1,1) weights of the components of chiral superfield and restored the multiplier
(\rho^{\#}_{\rm 2})^{2}
which was omitted for simplicity in Eqs.
(
3.27
), (
3.34
), (
3.35
) and (
3.36
) as well as in the above paragraph. The multiplier
(\rho^{\#}_{\rm 1})^{4}
relates the field
(\phi^{*})=\bar{\phi}
conjugate to complex axion–dilaton
\phi
of type IIB supergrvaity, which we identify with leading component of the superfield (
III.1
), with the highest component,
\bar{\phi}^{[+8]}
, of this superfield.
The role of such multipliers is to balance the SO(1,1) weights of
l.h.s
-s and
r.h.s
-s in
(
V.3
) and in some of the equations below.

To find the coefficient
{\cal B}_{3}
, which in this case should be assumed to be
\Theta
–independent,
we have to calculate the same amplitude from superamplitude (
5.51
)
22
22

22

In the notation similar to the one used for amplitudes in the
l.h.s.
of Eq. (
5.55
), this can be written as

{\cal A}_{3}(\Phi_{{\rm 1}}\Phi_{{\rm 2}}\Phi_{{\rm 3}})={\bf Q}^{{12}}{\cal B}_{3}\;
,
where under
\Phi_{{\rm i}}
the superfields (
III.1
) are understood. The statements that superamplitudes depend on superfields which can be found in some articles (for instance in
Elvang:2013cua
arround Eq. (4.33) of this paper) should be understood in this sense and not as real dependence of on-shell superamplitudes on the generic superfields

\Phi_{{\rm i}}=\Phi(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}},v^{-}_{{\rm i}},\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}})
. We avoid such type statements as they might produce a confusion.
.
This can be done by Grassmann integration
or equivalently by applying covariant derivatives and then setting all the

\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}}
equal to zero, i.e. from

A(\bar{\phi}\phi_{ABCD}\phi)=\left(\int d^{8}\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 1}}(d^{4}\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 2}})_{ABCD}\,{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{{12}}\,{\cal B}_{3}\right)|_{\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 2}}=0=\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 3}}}\equiv\left(((D^{+}_{{\rm 1}})^{\wedge 8}D^{+}_{A\,{\rm 2}}D^{+}_{B\,{\rm 2}}D^{+}_{C\,{\rm 2}}D^{+}_{D\,{\rm 2}}\,{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{{12}})\,{\cal B}_{3}\right)|_{\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}}=0}\;,

(5.58)

where
(D^{+}_{{\rm 1}})^{\wedge 8}:=\frac{1}{8!}\epsilon^{A_{1}\ldots A_{8}}D^{+}_{{\rm 1}A_{1}}\ldots D^{+}_{{\rm 1}A_{8}}
.

Grassmann integral in (
5.58
) extracts from

{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{{12}}
the coefficient for the terms with highest degree
of
\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 1}}
, fourth degree of
\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 2}}
and no
\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 3}}
. Separating the relevant term,

{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{{12}}=\frac{1}{4!\,8!}{\cal B}_{3}\tilde{s}{}^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{12}}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{1}{\rm 2}}\ldots{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{4}{\rm 2}}\;{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{5}{\rm 1}}\ldots{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{12}{\rm 1}}+...\;,\qquad

(5.59)

where

\tilde{s}{}^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{12}}:=\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{16}}\tilde{{\rm s}}_{\dot{q}_{1}\ldots\dot{q}_{4}}\,(\rho^{\#})^{-2}v^{+}_{\alpha_{13}\dot{q}_{1}}\ldots v^{+}_{\alpha_{16}\dot{q}_{4}}=\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{16}}\tilde{{\rm s}}{}^{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}\,(\rho^{\#})^{-2}\bar{v}{}^{+}_{\alpha_{13}A_{1}}\ldots\bar{v}{}^{+}_{\alpha_{16}A_{4}}\,,

(5.60)

we find that

\displaystyle\left((D^{+}_{{\rm 1}})^{\wedge 8}D^{+}_{{\rm 2}A}D^{+}_{{\rm 2}B}D^{+}_{{\rm 2}C}D^{+}_{{\rm 2}D}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{12}\right)|_{\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}}=0}=2^{24}\,4!\,(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{8}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 2}})^{4}(\rho^{\#})^{-2}\;|\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm 21}}|^{4}\;{\rm s}_{{}_{ABCD}}\qquad

\displaystyle=\frac{2^{21}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{8}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 2}})^{4}}{(\rho^{\#})^{2}}\,|\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm 21}}|^{-2}\;A(\bar{\phi}^{[+8]}\phi_{{}_{ABCD}}^{[+4]}\phi)\;,\qquad

(5.61)

where at the last stage Eq (
V.3
) was used.
See
Appendix
D.3
for details on derivation of this result
(which is straightforward but requires some experience in spinor moving frame approach).

Comparing (
V.3
) with (
5.58
), we find

{\cal B}_{3}=\frac{(\rho^{\#})^{2}}{2^{21}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{2}\,(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 2}})^{2}\,|\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm 21}}|^{2}}

(5.62)

which results in the following expression for three point superamplitude

{\cal A}_{3}={\hbox{\mybb Q}^{12}}\,(\rho^{\#})^{2}\,\frac{1}{2^{21}\,(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{2}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 2}})^{2}|\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm 21}}|^{2}}\;.

(5.63)

At first glance, one can be surprised by the presence in the superamplitude of the multiplier
(\rho^{\#})^{2}
which is the Langrange multiplier for
SO(1,1)
gauge symmetry of the reference moving frame and hence is clearly unphysical. However, a more careful look shows that this multiplier just cancels
(\rho^{\#})^{-2}

which was introduced in the definition (
5.50
) of
{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{12}
to make this gauge invariant.
The quantity independent on unphysical
(\rho^{\#})^{2}
has nonvanishing SO(1,1) weight and is given just by the product
{\hbox{\mybb Q}^{12}}\,(\rho^{\#})^{2}\,
, i.e. by

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{{12}\;[+4]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{12!}\,\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\ldots\alpha_{16}}{\tilde{{\rm s}}}{}^{A_{1}\ldots A_{4}}\,v^{+}_{\alpha_{1}A_{1}}\ldots v^{+}_{\alpha_{4}A_{4}}\,{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{5}}\ldots{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha_{16}}\;\;.\qquad

(5.64)

In terms of this covariant expression, the 3–point superamplitude (
5.63
) reads

{\cal A}_{3}={\hbox{\mybb Q}^{12\;[+4]}}\,\frac{1}{2^{21}\,(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 1}})^{2}(\rho^{\#}_{{\rm 2}})^{2}|\vec{K}^{=}_{{\rm 21}}|^{2}}\;.

(5.65)

Of course, the numerical coefficient
2^{-21}
in this expression is purely conventional: it comes from the coefficient 2 in the r.h.s. of supersymmetry algebra
(
5.21
) which results in coefficient 4 in the representation (
5.18
) for supersymmetry generators.

V.4
On on-shell amplitudes of type IIA supergravity

The fact that the state vector of type IIA superparticle, when quantized in our formalism, is described by the same analytic on-shell superfield as the type IIB one, suggests that the superamplitudes discussed in secs.
V.2
and
V.3
also describe the processes of scattering of type IIA supergravitons, i.e. of the members of type IIA supergravity multiplet.
In this section we will discuss the restrictions on such a description.

To begin with, let us notice that, when the unified description of type IIB and type IIA processes is valid, the
spacetime interpretation of type IIA amplitudes is a bit more convoluted and involves the covariantly constant
SO(8)
vector
k^{i}
(
4.32
) related to the complex Stückelberg fields defining the complex structure by (
4.29
) and (
4.30
).

In particular the amplitude extracted from superamplitude (
5.51
) as shown in (
5.58
), describes processes involving, besides a graviton
h^{ij}
and a dilaton
\phi^{IIA}
, also the a solution for
1
-form field of type IIA supermultiplet in contraction with this
8
-vector,
c^{i}k^{i}
(see (
4.49
)).
The part of this one-form, orthogonal to
k^{i}
, namely the combination
k^{[i}c^{j]}
, contributes, together with the projection of the type IIA
3
-form on
k^{i}
,
into the amplitude (see (
4.56
))

\displaystyle\left(\int d^{8}\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 1}}(d^{2}\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 2}})_{AB}\,{Q}^{{12}}\,{\cal B}_{3}\right)|_{\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 2}}=0=\Theta^{-}_{{\rm 3}}}=\left(((D^{+}_{{\rm 1}})^{\wedge 8}D^{+}_{A\,{\rm 2}}D^{+}_{B\,{\rm 2}}\,{Q}^{{12}})\,{\cal B}_{3}\right)|_{\Theta^{-}_{{\rm i}}=0}\;.

(5.66)

Notice that above we have not attributed

8
-vector
k^{i}
to a definite (super)particle
(i.e. did not supply it with some subindex
{\rm i}
, e.g.
{\rm 2}
) and this requires some explanation.
What is manifest (in particular from the discussion of supercharges below) is that for each particle (
\forall\;{\rm i}
) we have to use the same spacelike unit 10-vector
k_{\mu}
.
All momenta are orthogonal to the
T-duality direction defined by this vector,

k_{\mu}p^{\mu}_{{\rm i}}=0
, the fact which already puts a restrictions on the processes with 8 or more particles which can be described within our present formalism.

Our quantization of type IIA superparticle indicates that, when scattering process of type IIA supergavitons
is considered, the spacelike unit vector
k_{\mu}
has similar decomposition on each moving frame:

k_{\mu}=-k^{j}_{{\rm i}}u_{\mu{\rm i}}^{j}\;.

(5.67)

This decomposition apparently involves different unit eight-vector vectors
k^{j}_{{\rm i}}
which are transformed by different
SO(8)
groups: by
SO(8)_{{\rm i}}
acting on spinor moving frame of

{\rm i}
-th particle; these
k^{j}_{{\rm i}}
are covariantly constant with respect to this group,

dk^{j}_{{\rm i}}+k^{k}_{{\rm i}}\Omega^{kj}_{{\rm i}}=0
(see Appendix (
D.4
) for relation between Cartan forms of different particles).

However, in the gauge (
5.6
)–(
5.8
) it can be shown (see Appendix
D.4
) that actually
all
k^{j}_{{\rm i}}=k_{\mu}u^{\mu j}_{{\rm i}}
coincide with
k^{j}=k_{\mu}u^{\mu j}
, so that

k_{\mu}=-k^{j}u_{\mu}^{j}\;=\;-k^{j}u_{\mu{\rm i}}^{j}\qquad\forall{\rm i}\;.

(5.68)

Also the form of complex supercharges used to construct superamplitude in type IIA case requires some comments.
From the definition of the complex coordinate of the analytical basis (
4.28
), we find that the rigid supersymmetry transformations act on that as

\displaystyle\delta_{susy}\Theta^{-A}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{w}_{q}^{A}(\epsilon^{1\alpha}v^{\;-}_{\alpha q}+ik\!\!\!/{}_{q\dot{p}}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\epsilon^{2-}_{\alpha})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\epsilon^{1\alpha}v^{\;-}_{\alpha q}{w}_{q}^{A}+i\epsilon^{2-}_{\alpha}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}{w}_{\dot{q}}^{A})\;.\qquad

(5.69)

This determines the corresponding derivative terms in the
representation of supersymmetry generators defined by

\delta_{susy}=\epsilon^{1\alpha}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{1}_{\alpha}+\epsilon^{2-}_{\alpha}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2\alpha}
to be

{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{1}_{\alpha}=v^{\;-}_{\alpha q}{w}_{{q}}^{A}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta^{-A}}+...
and
{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2\alpha}=iv^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}{w}_{\dot{q}}^{A}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta^{-A}}+...
.
The remaining terms linear in
\Theta^{-A}
, can be determined
from type IIA supersymmetry algebra

\displaystyle{}\{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{1}_{\alpha},{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{1}_{\beta}\}\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2p_{\mu}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}=\;4\rho^{\#}v^{\;-}_{\alpha q}v^{\;-}_{\beta q}=\;4\rho^{\#}v^{\;-A}_{\alpha}\bar{v}{}^{\;-}_{\beta A}\;,\qquad

(5.70)

\displaystyle{}\{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{1}_{\alpha},{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2\beta}\}\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;0\;,\qquad

(5.71)

\displaystyle{}\{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2\alpha},{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2\beta}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2p_{\mu}\tilde{\sigma}^{\mu\,,\alpha\beta}=4\rho^{\#}v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}v^{-\beta}_{\dot{q}}=4\rho^{\#}v^{-\alpha A}\,\bar{v}_{A}^{-\beta}\;,\qquad

(5.72)

where at the last stages we have used (
4.27
). In such a way we find

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{1}_{\alpha}=\;v^{\;-}_{\alpha q}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{1}_{q}=\;v^{\;-}_{\alpha q}\left({w}_{{q}}^{A}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta^{-A}}+4\rho^{\#}\bar{w}_{qA}\Theta^{-A}\right)\;,\qquad

(5.73)

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2\alpha}=v^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2}_{\dot{q}}=iv^{-\alpha}_{\dot{q}}\left({w}_{\dot{q}}^{A}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta^{-A}}-4\rho^{\#}\bar{w}_{\dot{q}A}\Theta^{-A}\right)\;.\qquad

(5.74)

Now let us contract
{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2\alpha}
with

k\!\!\!/{}_{\alpha\beta}=k^{i}u_{\mu}^{i}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\beta}=k^{i}\gamma^{i}_{q\dot{p}}(v^{-}_{\alpha q}v^{+}_{\beta\dot{p}}+v^{+}_{\alpha\dot{p}}v^{-}_{\beta q})\;,

(5.75)

where
k^{i}
is the unit covariantly constant eight-vector (
4.25
) used to define the complex structure in type IIA superparticle model through Eqs. (
4.29
), (
4.30
) and (
4.31
). This gives

k\!\!\!/{}_{\alpha\beta}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2\beta}=iv_{\alpha q}^{-}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}^{2}_{{q}}=v_{\alpha q}^{-}\,i\,\left({w}_{{q}}^{A}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta^{-A}}-4\rho^{\#}\bar{w}_{{q}A}\Theta^{-A}\right)\;,

(5.76)

so that the complex generators

\displaystyle\bar{{\hbox{\mybb Q}}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{1}-ik\!\!\!/{}_{\alpha\beta}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\rm i}^{2\beta})=v_{\alpha q\,{\rm i}}^{\;-}w_{q{\rm i}}^{A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta_{{\rm i}}^{-A}}=v_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{-A}\frac{\partial}{\partial\Theta_{{\rm i}}^{-A}}\;,\qquad

(5.77)

\displaystyle{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}({\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\alpha{\rm i}}^{1}+ik\!\!\!/{}_{\alpha\beta}{\hbox{\mybb Q}}_{\rm i}^{2\beta})=4\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}v_{\alpha q\,{\rm i}}^{\;-}\bar{w}_{qA{\rm i}}\Theta_{{\rm i}}^{-A}=4\rho^{\#}_{{\rm i}}\bar{v}_{\alpha A{\rm i}}^{\;-}\Theta_{{\rm i}}^{-A}

(5.78)

have exactly the same representation in terms of complex coordinates
\Theta^{-A}
as those for type IIB case.

Notice that in this last equation we have restored the subindex
{\rm i}
enumerating points (superparticles) in the scattering process. However, as we have discussed above, for all
{\rm i}
we can use the same covariantly constant
8
-vector
k^{i}
after the gauge (
5.6
)–(
5.8
). Then

w_{q{\rm i}}^{A}
with different values of

{\rm i}
can differ by
SU(8)
transformations only. Furthermore, we can fix the gauge when all these coincide,
w_{q{\rm i}}^{A}=w_{q}^{A}
and this gauge is preserved by a single
SU(8)
symmetry acting on the reference bridge variable
w_{q}^{A}
.

It is also important to notice that in the gauge (
5.6
)–(
5.8
) we can also find

\vec{k}{\vec{{\hbox{\mybb K}}}}_{{\rm i}^{\prime}{\rm i}}:=\vec{k}({\vec{{\hbox{\mybb K}}}}_{{\rm i}^{\prime}}-{\vec{{\hbox{\mybb K}}}}_{{\rm i}})=0\qquad\forall\;{\rm i}^{\prime},{\rm i}^{\prime}\;,

(5.79)

which follows from

\vec{k}{\vec{{\hbox{\mybb K}}}}_{{\rm i}}=-k^{=}:=-k_{\mu}u^{\mu=}\;.

(5.80)

If in our reference frame
k^{=}:=k_{\mu}u^{\mu=}=0
, we have even more illustrative relations

\vec{k}\vec{{\hbox{\mybb K}}}_{{\rm i}}=0\qquad\forall\;{\rm i}\;.

(5.81)

This makes clear that in the case of process
which contains
seven or less of type IIA supergravitons, we can always choose the T-duality direction in such a way that the condition (
5.81
) holds.
Thus, the use of a special T-duality direction to define complex structure can put restrictions on processes with more than seven type IIA supergravitons which can be described by our formalism.

This is clearly better than the restriction put by using for
10
or
11
dimensional processes the D=4 formalism which restricts the possibility of generic studies to processes with four or less particles (see e.g.
Herderschee:2019ofc
).

Thus, at least the amplitudes for processed with seven or less type IIA supergravitons can be read off the superamplitudes found for its type IIB cousin with the above correspondence originated in T-duality. Notice that at the level of linearized supergravity the necessity of compactness of the T-duality direction is not manifest.

V.5
Towards on-shell amplitudes of type IIA string theory involving D
0
branes

In type IIA string theory, besides the scattering of
supergravitons, there are also processes involving supergravity multiplets and D
0
–branes, which are massive superparticles in type IIA superspace. An interesting problem is then what are
superamplitudes
for such processes. The issue is not trivial: to our best knowledge, even in
D=4
its counterpart was addressed only recently and only for
{\cal N}=1
case
Herderschee:2019ofc
;
Delgado:2025oev
.
Below we will discuss some first stages towards such superamplitudes in
D=10
type IIA string theory and indicate an issue hampering the straightforward generalization of the known ways to solve the Ward identities in this case.

V.5.1
Spinor helicity variables, on-shell superfield and quantum state spectrum of D
0
brane

The quantization of spinor moving frame formulation of D
0
–brane was elaborated recently in
Bandos:2025pxv
and resulted in a state vector described by on-shell superfield quite similar to (
III.1
):

\displaystyle\Xi_{{}_{{\text{D0}}}}(x^{0},\Theta^{A},\bar{\Theta}_{A};v,w)

\displaystyle=
